Table 4
Commonsense and Adaptive Decision-Making
1. Q: What does commonsense mean in the framework presented in Chapter 4 of Primal Intelligence?
A: Show answer
In Angus Fletcher's framework, commonsense is the capacity to recognize when a familiar understanding or established plan may no longer fit the situation. It helps a person distinguish between circumstances that support continuing a reliable course of action and circumstances that require reassessment. Rather than requiring complete information before acting, commonsense monitors whether the conditions supporting a decision remain dependable. When unexpected developments challenge existing assumptions, it signals the need to investigate, consider alternatives, and potentially change direction. The framework presents commonsense as a practical decision-making capability that combines confidence in proven approaches with sensitivity to emerging exceptions. It is not simply conventional wisdom or the ability to repeat familiar rules; it includes knowing when those rules may no longer work.

Study instruction:
- Explain how commonsense can support both consistency and adaptation, using a decision from work or everyday life.
Key point:
- Commonsense helps determine when established plans remain appropriate.
- Recognizing changing conditions is as important as understanding existing rules.
2. Q: How does commonsense differ from relying exclusively on formal logic and historical data?
A: Show answer
Formal logic and historical data can identify patterns, test relationships, and support consistent calculations. However, a conclusion based on past observations may become unreliable when circumstances change. Fletcher contrasts a primarily rule-based approach with a form of judgment that actively looks for evidence that the present situation differs from the conditions under which a rule previously succeeded. This does not mean that logic or historical data are useless. They remain valuable when their assumptions are appropriate. The practical distinction is between using information to support a decision and assuming that existing information fully describes reality. A well-designed decision process combines analytical evidence with checks for changed assumptions, new risks, and conditions that existing models may not represent.

Study instruction:
- Compare a decision based on historical averages with one that also tests whether the underlying conditions have changed.
Key point:
- Historical evidence is useful but does not guarantee future outcomes.
- Analytical methods should be reviewed when their assumptions become unreliable.
3. Q: What are unknown unknowns, and why do they matter in decision-making?
A: Show answer
Unknown unknowns are relevant factors, events, or possibilities that a decision-maker has not identified as missing from their understanding. They differ from known unknowns, which are uncertainties that a person can already describe, such as an unresolved delivery date or an unconfirmed budget. Unknown unknowns are harder to manage because they are not yet represented in a checklist, forecast, or risk register. Fletcher argues that sensitivity to unexpected developments helps people recognize the limitations of their existing understanding. In practice, an organization cannot list every conceivable uncertainty in advance. It can, however, monitor unexpected results, conflicting evidence, new circumstances, and repeated exceptions. These signals should prompt an examination of assumptions rather than automatic application of the existing procedure.

Study instruction:
- Create two examples of known unknowns and two examples of unknown unknowns in a project, and explain how each could be detected.
Key point:
- Known unknowns can be documented in advance; unknown unknowns require active detection.
- Unexpected evidence can reveal gaps in the current understanding.
4. Q: How does narrative thinking help people recognize the limits of their understanding?
A: Show answer
Fletcher connects commonsense to narrative thinking, particularly the use of causal questions and imagined possibilities. A person considers why something is happening and what might happen under different conditions. If the available explanations no longer account for observations, or plausible future outcomes conflict with the current plan, the person may recognize that their understanding is incomplete. This approach differs from treating a decision as a calculation performed in a fixed environment. Narrative thinking places events in a changing sequence, allowing previous experiences to inform expectations while leaving room for unexpected developments. Its practical value is not that every imagined possibility is accurate, but that discrepancies between expectations and reality can trigger a useful review of the decision.

Study instruction:
- Take an unexpected workplace outcome and write down the original explanation, the evidence that challenges it, and two alternative explanations.
Key point:
- Causal explanations help people identify when their current model no longer fits the evidence.
- Imagined alternatives should be tested against actual developments.
5. Q: What is environmental volatility, and how should it influence a decision?
A: Show answer
Environmental volatility describes how much relevant conditions change or how uncertain their future behavior becomes. In Fletcher's model, changing conditions increase the likelihood that a familiar rule will encounter an exception. When an environment is relatively stable, established procedures and accumulated experience may provide a sound basis for action. When significant changes appear, decision-makers should examine whether the old assumptions still hold and whether different approaches are required. Volatility is not synonymous with disorder, and a single unexpected event does not necessarily prove that an entire system has changed. The important task is to distinguish temporary variation from a meaningful shift in conditions. That distinction should determine whether to continue, monitor, investigate, or revise a plan.

Study instruction:
- Choose a familiar process and identify three indicators that would suggest its operating conditions have materially changed.
Key point:
- Stable conditions often favor reliable established procedures.
- Changing conditions justify closer scrutiny of existing assumptions.
6. Q: Why should confidence in a plan depend on more than the amount of evidence supporting it?
A: Show answer
A large amount of historical evidence can increase confidence, but it may not reveal whether the conditions that produced those results still exist. A plan can appear well supported because the analysis excludes events or relationships that were not previously considered. Fletcher's approach emphasizes looking for meaningful reasons that the plan may no longer fit the environment. Operationally, this means checking key assumptions, investigating surprising results, and identifying circumstances that would invalidate the decision. The absence of an obvious warning is not proof that a plan is correct; it is one input into judgment alongside evidence, expertise, monitoring, and the consequences of being wrong. Decisions with severe downside risks may require formal safeguards even when current conditions appear stable.

Study instruction:
- Identify the main assumptions behind a decision and specify what evidence would cause you to reconsider each assumption.
Key point:
- Confidence should reflect the quality and continuing relevance of evidence.
- Important assumptions need explicit tests and monitoring.
7. Q: Why does Fletcher emphasize maintaining a bias toward action while remaining alert to exceptions?
A: Show answer
Many practical tasks require action before every uncertainty can be resolved. Waiting for complete certainty may create unnecessary delays, especially when a reasonable procedure already has a record of success. Fletcher describes commonsense as supporting continued action under familiar conditions while retaining the capacity to interrupt that action when meaningful warnings arise. The balance is important: acting without any monitoring can become reckless, while repeatedly reconsidering a sound decision can become paralysis. A practical system establishes a reasonable default course, defines conditions that warrant review, and specifies who can pause or change the plan. This preserves operational momentum without treating an existing approach as permanently correct.

Study instruction:
- Draft a decision rule that identifies the normal course of action, the warning indicators, and the circumstances requiring escalation.
Key point:
- Action and vigilance should operate together.
- Clear exception criteria reduce both impulsive changes and unnecessary hesitation.
8. Q: How can a decision-maker distinguish a temporary anomaly from a fundamental change?
A: Show answer
A temporary anomaly is an unusual observation that does not necessarily undermine the underlying assumptions of a plan. A fundamental change affects the conditions on which the plan depends, potentially making an established method ineffective. A single unexpected result should therefore prompt investigation rather than an automatic overhaul. Decision-makers can compare the observation with independent evidence, examine whether related indicators have changed, assess the likely cause, and monitor whether the pattern continues. They should also consider the cost of waiting versus the cost of reacting prematurely. In a safety-critical environment, an initial anomaly may justify an immediate precaution even before its cause is confirmed. In other circumstances, controlled monitoring may provide sufficient information before committing to a major change.

Study instruction:
- Develop a short case where an unusual result could be either a temporary anomaly or a lasting change, then list the evidence needed to distinguish them.
Key point:
- An anomaly requires interpretation, not automatic dismissal or automatic escalation.
- Response speed should reflect the evidence and the potential consequences of delay.
9. Q: What can Benjamin Franklin's approach to learning teach about commonsense?
A: Show answer
Fletcher presents Franklin as an example of combining respect for established wisdom with openness to new discoveries. Franklin learned through reading, practical activity, observation, and participation in public life rather than relying exclusively on formal education. His career crossed several fields, illustrating how knowledge can be developed through repeated engagement with real problems. The chapter uses his maxims to illustrate the importance of preserving reliable habits while adapting when circumstances demand it. The lesson is not that traditional advice is always correct. Rather, accumulated experience provides a useful starting point, while experimentation and observation help establish when a different approach is warranted. The approach also underscores the value of continually learning from sources beyond one's immediate professional specialization.

Study instruction:
- Select one established principle you follow and one recent experience that might justify refining how you apply it.
Key point:
- Experience and experimentation can complement each other.
- Reliable wisdom should inform judgment without preventing new learning.
10. Q: Why is memorizing wise sayings insufficient to develop practical commonsense?
A: Show answer
A maxim can express an insightful principle, but applying it requires determining whether the present situation fits the circumstances for which the principle is useful. Someone may memorize advice about patience yet fail to recognize when a decision requires urgency. Another person may value innovation but overlook evidence that a reliable existing procedure is preferable. Fletcher's discussion illustrates the difference between knowing a principle and developing the judgment needed to use it appropriately. Practical development requires experience, feedback, observation, and repeated decisions in conditions that vary in familiarity and uncertainty. Learners should therefore examine not only what a maxim recommends, but also what assumptions it depends on, when it may fail, and what evidence should guide its application.

Study instruction:
- Choose a familiar maxim and identify one situation where it is helpful, one where it may be harmful, and the evidence that distinguishes them.
Key point:
- Principles require contextual judgment.
- Skill develops through application and feedback, not memorization alone.
11. Q: How does the chapter propose developing commonsense through training?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a training approach that alternates between routine and unfamiliar situations. Routine practice helps learners recognize dependable procedures, while novelty challenges them to notice when familiar expectations no longer explain what is happening. The purpose is to develop sensitivity to the boundaries of existing knowledge and to improve the ability to respond when conditions change. Fletcher presents this training through a special-operations setting, but the general learning principle can also be applied to business, education, project management, and other environments. A useful adaptation should combine realistic scenarios, observable performance criteria, feedback, and progressively more complex problems. Training should evaluate whether the learner recognizes a meaningful exception and responds appropriately rather than simply testing memory for instructions.

Study instruction:
- Design one routine exercise and one unexpected scenario for the same task, with measurable criteria for recognizing when the procedure needs review.
Key point:
- Routine practice builds dependable responses; novelty tests their limits.
- Effective training combines realistic practice, feedback, and evaluation.
12. Q: What role does anxiety play in Fletcher's model of commonsense?
A: Show answer
Fletcher treats anxiety as a potential signal that the future contains unresolved possibilities or threats that the current plan may not address. In this framework, a moderate level of concern can direct attention toward uncertainty, encourage preparation, and make a person more alert to changes. Anxiety is therefore not automatically interpreted as evidence of poor judgment. However, the chapter's conceptual model should not be taken to mean that all anxiety is beneficial or that distress reliably measures actual risk. Anxiety can arise from many sources and can become disruptive. A practical application is to examine what the concern is signaling, separate evidence-based risks from imagined possibilities, and choose a proportionate response. Persistent or severe anxiety that affects functioning may warrant professional support rather than further self-monitoring alone.

Study instruction:
- Record a recent concern, its evidence, the uncertainty involved, and one proportionate action that could reduce the uncertainty.
Key point:
- Concern can prompt useful investigation when interpreted carefully.
- The intensity of anxiety is not, by itself, a reliable measure of objective danger.
13. Q: How can someone distinguish useful vigilance from excessive worry?
A: Show answer
Useful vigilance directs attention toward risks that can be investigated, prepared for, or managed. Excessive worry often spreads across many possible futures without producing proportionate decisions or useful new information. Fletcher's training framework emphasizes tuning anxiety rather than trying to eliminate every concern. In practical terms, the individual can define the problem, evaluate its likelihood and consequences, identify controllable factors, and establish a specific next action. Concerns that cannot be resolved immediately can be recorded for review instead of repeatedly reconsidered. The aim is to retain awareness without allowing hypothetical outcomes to dominate current work. This is a decision-management technique, not a substitute for clinical assessment where anxiety is persistent, overwhelming, or impairing daily life.

Study instruction:
- For three worries, identify which facts are known, which possibilities remain uncertain, and what action or review date is appropriate.
Key point:
- Useful vigilance is connected to evidence and proportionate action.
- Repeated worry without a decision or new information may require a different response.
14. Q: What does the distinction between past anxiety and future anxiety mean?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's framework, past anxiety arises when current concern is connected to something that has already gone wrong. The person may fear a repetition of a previous mistake or mentally revisit an alternative outcome. Future anxiety, by contrast, concerns an event that has not yet occurred and may introduce a new challenge. Fletcher argues that these two kinds of concern should lead to different responses. A past problem can often be addressed by learning from the event and improving the procedure. A genuinely new possibility calls for vigilance, preparation, and monitoring. This distinction prevents every past failure from becoming a reason to avoid an activity, while also reducing the risk of ignoring unfamiliar threats. The categories are a practical framework for reflection rather than a formal clinical classification of anxiety.

Study instruction:
- Classify five current concerns as primarily related to a previous event or a genuinely new future possibility, and choose a different response for each type.
Key point:
- Past-oriented concerns can guide learning and procedural improvement.
- Future-oriented concerns can guide preparation for new possibilities.
15. Q: How should a past mistake be converted into a standard operating procedure?
A: Show answer
A standard operating procedure, or SOP, records a repeatable approach intended to produce a dependable result. When an unwanted event occurs, the learner should reconstruct what happened, identify the contributing conditions, determine whether the existing procedure failed or was applied incorrectly, and evaluate a practical change. The revised SOP should address the identified cause without introducing unnecessary complexity. It should then be documented, communicated, and tested in appropriate conditions. Fletcher uses this process to illustrate how a past concern can become useful preparation rather than a continuing source of avoidance. A single adverse result does not always justify changing a reliable procedure, especially when the event was genuinely unusual. The decision should consider evidence, risk, and whether the modification would improve future outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Write a brief incident review containing the original procedure, the failure mechanism, the proposed change, approval requirements, and a test of the revised procedure.
Key point:
- Learn from previous failures by improving the process where evidence supports a change.
- SOP changes should be documented and tested before becoming standard practice.
16. Q: When should a reliable procedure be retained despite a previous bad outcome?
A: Show answer
A previous failure does not necessarily prove that the procedure itself was defective. The event may have resulted from unusual conditions, a one-time external disruption, inadequate preparation, or incorrect execution. Before replacing the established approach, decision-makers should examine the cause, the procedure's broader performance record, and the expected consequences of changing it. If a modification offers no demonstrated improvement, preserving the existing procedure may be preferable while documenting the exception and monitoring for recurrence. In safety-critical work, even a rare failure can justify redesign when its consequences are severe. The relevant question is therefore not whether a procedure has ever failed, but whether the available evidence supports continuing it, strengthening its safeguards, or replacing it.

Study instruction:
- Analyze one failed outcome and distinguish a procedure defect from an execution problem or an exceptional external event.
Key point:
- A single bad outcome should trigger investigation rather than automatic rejection of a process.
- The consequences of failure must influence the decision to retain or revise a procedure.
17. Q: What does the principle of focusing on the near future contribute to commonsense?
A: Show answer
Fletcher expresses this principle as concentrating on the next meaningful step rather than attempting to anticipate every distant outcome. The chapter calls this focus "Now + 1." The next stage is often sufficiently close to permit useful preparation and observation, while more distant outcomes depend on many intermediate events that may change. A manager, for example, can prepare for the next project milestone and define responses to likely obstacles without pretending to predict the entire project's course. This approach helps prevent planning from becoming an endless exercise in hypothetical possibilities. It also preserves the ability to update later decisions when new information arrives. The method does not eliminate long-term strategy; it connects that strategy to a sequence of nearer decisions that can be observed and adjusted.

Study instruction:
- For a current goal, define the next action, two plausible obstacles, the evidence that would trigger a response, and the next review point.
Key point:
- Focus near-term analysis on events that can be influenced or monitored.
- Reassess distant possibilities as intermediate conditions develop.
18. Q: How can a person prepare for possible problems without reacting to events that have not occurred?
A: Show answer
Preparation and implementation are separate decisions. A person can identify a plausible problem, develop a response, and establish criteria for taking action without immediately changing course. This preserves readiness while allowing evidence to determine whether the response is needed. For example, a project manager might prepare a contingency supplier but retain the current supplier until delivery indicators show a genuine risk. An appropriate trigger should be specific enough to guide a decision rather than invite endless interpretation. The organization should also define who monitors the trigger and who authorizes the response. In emergencies or situations where delay could cause serious harm, immediate protective action may be appropriate; the principle is to match the response to the risk rather than follow a rigid rule of waiting.

Study instruction:
- Create a contingency plan with a clearly defined trigger, responsible decision-maker, implementation step, and evidence requirement.
Key point:
- Preparedness does not require premature implementation.
- Response thresholds should reflect the urgency and consequences of the risk.
19. Q: How do past learning and future vigilance work together?
A: Show answer
Past learning helps create reliable procedures based on experience, while future vigilance helps a person detect circumstances that those procedures may not cover. If attention is devoted entirely to the past, a decision-maker may repeatedly anticipate previous failures and become reluctant to act. If attention is devoted only to possible future developments, the person may disregard valuable experience and continually redesign procedures without adequate reason. Fletcher's framework combines the two by first processing lessons from known events and then directing attention toward the next stage of activity. The resulting approach preserves the benefits of established knowledge while maintaining readiness for emerging conditions. This balance is valuable in operational environments where repeated processes must remain efficient but cannot be assumed to be immune to change.

Study instruction:
- Use one recurring process to identify a lesson from a past exception and a new indicator to monitor during the next cycle.
Key point:
- Experience supports stable execution; vigilance supports adaptation.
- Neither past lessons nor future possibilities should be considered in isolation.
20. Q: How does Warren Buffett's experience illustrate the use of past anxiety?
A: Show answer
Fletcher uses Buffett's public-speaking difficulties to illustrate how an earlier negative experience can be converted into a repeatable procedure. Rather than allowing fear of speaking to dictate permanent avoidance, Buffett participated in training that emphasized preparation and a practiced approach to speaking. The example illustrates the broader principle that a failed experience can provide information about what needs to change. The appropriate response is not necessarily to eliminate all discomfort before attempting the task. It can be to develop methods that make the task manageable, practice them, and evaluate their effectiveness. The chapter also connects this type of learning to Buffett's investing philosophy, presenting attention to value and disciplined responses to market fear as examples of applying established principles without treating past emotional reactions as infallible guides.

Study instruction:
- Identify a task you avoid because of a previous failure and design a practical procedure that addresses the specific cause of that failure.
Key point:
- Past discomfort can become useful information for preparing a better response.
- Repeatable routines can support performance in challenging situations.
21. Q: What investment lesson does the chapter draw from Benjamin Graham and Warren Buffett?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts efforts to predict future stock prices from historical patterns with an approach that emphasizes the value available at the current price. In the account presented by Fletcher, Graham's influence encouraged Buffett to focus on buying assets at attractive prices rather than assuming that sophisticated calculations could reliably forecast every future movement. The broader lesson is to distinguish a decision's current merits from confidence in a prediction about what will happen next. This does not mean historical analysis or valuation models should be abandoned. It means their results must be interpreted with awareness of uncertainty, changing conditions, and the risk of overconfidence. Investment decisions still require independent analysis of financial information, asset quality, valuation, diversification, liquidity, and the investor's objectives and risk tolerance.

Study instruction:
- Compare an investment thesis based on expected future price movements with one based on current valuation, assumptions, and downside risk.
Key point:
- A valuation decision and a prediction about future price are different judgments.
- Confidence in a model should not replace risk assessment and independent analysis.
22. Q: How does the market example show the danger of allowing past fear to determine present decisions?
A: Show answer
Fletcher's discussion suggests that a market participant may react to a current decline partly through memories of earlier market losses. When that reaction overwhelms an independent assessment of current conditions, an investor may sell without adequately evaluating the asset's present value or the underlying reasons for the decline. The same mechanism can operate outside investment: people may reject a reasonable opportunity because it resembles a previous failure. The corrective is not to ignore warning signs or assume that every decline presents a bargain. It is to separate the evidence about the current situation from the emotional impact of earlier events, then apply an explicit decision process. In financial markets, this should be supported by appropriate valuation methods, liquidity planning, risk limits, and recognition that prices can decline further.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a hypothetical market decline and list the evidence needed to distinguish a temporary loss of confidence from a deterioration in underlying value.
Key point:
- Previous losses can influence current risk perception.
- Decisions should distinguish emotional reactions from current evidence without dismissing genuine risk.
23. Q: What does the James Simons example illustrate about responding to new market information?
A: Show answer
Fletcher presents Simons as an example of adapting an analytical investment approach when market behavior no longer supports it. In the chapter's account, an initially successful mathematical model began to perform poorly, creating a decision about whether to continue trusting it or reconsider its assumptions. The example illustrates why a system's historical success is not a guarantee of continued validity. A responsible response includes monitoring performance, checking for data or implementation problems, reviewing whether market relationships have changed, and comparing alternatives. It also requires discipline: a short period of poor performance does not automatically invalidate a model, and a model should not be changed solely to avoid uncomfortable results. Evidence, testing, risk exposure, and defined review thresholds should guide the decision.

Study instruction:
- Design a model-monitoring checklist that distinguishes ordinary performance variation from deterioration requiring a formal review.
Key point:
- Previously successful models require ongoing monitoring.
- Revisions should be justified by evidence, not by fear or untested optimism.
24. Q: Why can an algorithm continue producing poor decisions after the conditions that supported it have changed?
A: Show answer
An algorithm generally operates according to its design, data, objectives, and assumptions. If the environment changes in a way that the model does not represent, its outputs may become less useful even though the calculations remain internally consistent. For example, a forecasting system may rely on relationships that no longer hold after a major change in customer behavior or supply conditions. The failure is not necessarily a mathematical error; it may be a mismatch between the model and its current operating environment. Good practice includes monitoring prediction errors, detecting changes in input data, reviewing assumptions, and defining escalation criteria. Decision-makers should also distinguish model failure from data-quality problems, software defects, and temporary noise before deciding whether to recalibrate, suspend, or replace the system.

Study instruction:
- Choose a predictive model and identify its critical assumptions, likely sources of drift, performance indicators, and conditions requiring human review.
Key point:
- Correct calculations can still produce poor decisions when assumptions no longer fit reality.
- Model monitoring must cover data, performance, context, and operational consequences.
25. Q: How should human judgment and automated decision systems complement one another?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that analytical systems can be particularly useful for routine situations and structured calculations, while human judgment is needed to question assumptions and interpret unfamiliar developments. A practical implementation should allocate responsibility according to the strengths and limitations of each method rather than assume either one is universally superior. Automation can process large volumes, apply consistent rules, and identify deviations. Human reviewers can investigate unusual outcomes, consider contextual information absent from the model, and authorize changes when required. Human judgment also needs controls because people can be biased, inconsistent, or overconfident. A reliable combined process defines monitoring responsibilities, exception thresholds, review procedures, override permissions, and evidence requirements. The objective is not to replace analysis with intuition, but to use each capability appropriately and maintain accountability for the final decision.

Study instruction:
- Map one routine decision workflow and specify which activities are automated, which exceptions require human review, and who authorizes overrides.
Key point:
- Automation supports consistency and scale; human oversight helps interpret exceptions.
- Both automated and human decisions require testing, monitoring, and accountability.
26. Q: How can an organization apply the chapter's framework to project management?
A: Show answer
A project team can begin with a documented plan based on available evidence, agreed responsibilities, and realistic assumptions. It can then identify leading indicators that would suggest changing conditions, such as supplier delays, unexpected costs, declining participation, or regulatory changes. Team members should distinguish problems that can be addressed by improving the established process from new developments that require a different response. Focusing on the next milestone helps the team define practical contingencies without creating an unmanageable list of remote possibilities. The team should record decisions, assess changes against agreed criteria, and review outcomes after each milestone. Significant scope, budget, or safety changes may require formal approval rather than informal adaptation. This approach combines consistent execution with controlled flexibility.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a project risk review that identifies the next milestone, three relevant warning indicators, escalation thresholds, and authorized contingency actions.
Key point:
- Project plans need both dependable procedures and defined adaptation mechanisms.
- Material changes should be documented and approved according to governance requirements.
27. Q: How can a team use commonsense without allowing personal intuition to bypass controls?
A: Show answer
Practical judgment should complement established controls rather than create an informal exception to them. When a team member suspects that a routine procedure no longer fits the situation, the concern should be supported by observations, evidence, and an explanation of the risk. The team can then use the appropriate escalation process to evaluate alternative actions. In environments with legal, safety, financial, or donor requirements, an intuitive concern does not authorize the team to ignore mandatory procedures. At the same time, blindly following a checklist can be inappropriate if circumstances indicate that the checklist is incomplete or a serious danger exists. Organizations should define how unusual situations are reported, who evaluates them, what emergency safeguards apply, and how decisions are recorded and reviewed afterward.

Study instruction:
- Draft an exception-escalation procedure that allows concerns to be raised promptly while preserving approval requirements and an audit trail.
Key point:
- Judgment should trigger disciplined review, not uncontrolled circumvention.
- Exception handling must preserve accountability and applicable obligations.
28. Q: What are the common failure modes when people try to improve decision-making under uncertainty?
A: Show answer
Several recurring problems can weaken judgment. One is excessive reliance on historical patterns when the environment has materially changed. Another is overreacting to a single unusual event and discarding a process that remains sound. A third is allowing old fears to dictate present decisions without investigating their causes. People can also become overwhelmed by distant hypothetical outcomes, delaying the next practical action. In automated environments, confidence in a model may discourage appropriate intervention when its outputs deteriorate. These failure modes require different remedies: review assumptions, investigate exceptions, learn from past events, focus on the next actionable stage, and establish clear monitoring thresholds. The aim is not to eliminate uncertainty, but to manage it in ways that maintain useful action and reduce avoidable errors.

Study instruction:
- For each failure mode, specify an early warning sign and one practical control that could prevent or mitigate it.
Key point:
- Overconfidence, overreaction, and hesitation can all weaken decisions.
- Effective safeguards should address the cause of each failure rather than apply one generic rule.
29. Q: How should a decision-maker respond when available evidence is incomplete or conflicting?
A: Show answer
The first step is to separate what is known from what remains uncertain. Conflicting evidence may reflect different time periods, inconsistent definitions, poor data quality, competing explanations, or genuine changes in the underlying situation. The decision-maker should identify which uncertainty matters most to the decision and whether additional evidence can realistically be obtained. They should then establish an interim course of action that reflects the consequences of being wrong, the urgency of the situation, and any applicable constraints. Where the risk is material, safeguards or independent review may be necessary. The plan should specify what new evidence would cause reconsideration. This creates a disciplined way to proceed without pretending that incomplete information provides certainty.

Study instruction:
- Build a decision log containing known facts, disputed evidence, key assumptions, interim action, risk controls, and conditions for reassessment.
Key point:
- Incomplete evidence requires explicit uncertainty management, not unsupported certainty.
- Decisions should include criteria for review when new information becomes available.
30. Q: How can managers determine whether their decision process is adapting appropriately to changing conditions?
A: Show answer
Managers should evaluate both the quality of routine execution and the handling of exceptions. Useful indicators include whether established procedures are followed when appropriate, whether significant deviations are detected promptly, how quickly unresolved issues are escalated, and whether corrective actions address verified causes. They should also examine whether decisions are being changed too frequently or retained despite evidence of deterioration. The evaluation should consider outcomes, but it should not treat every unfavorable result as proof that the original decision was unreasonable. A sound decision can still produce a bad outcome under uncertainty. Reviewing assumptions, available evidence, response timing, and subsequent learning provides a more complete picture of the decision process than outcome measures alone.

Study instruction:
- Develop a quarterly review using indicators for routine compliance, exception detection, response time, repeat failures, and corrective-action completion.
Key point:
- Effective adaptation requires evidence of both disciplined execution and timely reassessment.
- Decision quality should be assessed using the information available at the time, not outcomes alone.
31. Q: How does commonsense relate to intuition, imagination, and emotion in Fletcher's broader model of Primal Intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fletcher presents these four capabilities as connected parts of a broader approach to intelligent action. Intuition helps notice significant or unusual information. Imagination develops possible explanations, strategies, and future courses of action. Emotion provides feedback about experience and personal significance. Commonsense helps determine when the current approach remains suitable and when it should be reconsidered. The chapter's central contribution is to show that good judgment is not produced by one isolated ability. Generating possibilities without judging their relevance can lead to impractical plans; following established procedures without noticing change can lead to rigidity. A balanced decision process uses these capabilities together while checking interpretations against evidence, experience, and the requirements of the situation.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a recent decision and identify what you noticed, what alternatives you imagined, what feedback you experienced, and what caused you to retain or revise the plan.
Key point:
- Primal Intelligence combines several complementary decision-making capabilities.
- Commonsense helps connect established knowledge with context-sensitive action.
32. Q: What practical routine can help develop commonsense over time?
A: Show answer
A useful routine combines preparation, deliberate action, monitoring, and reflection. Before acting, identify the established procedure and the assumptions that make it suitable. Consider the most important near-term obstacle and prepare a proportionate response. During execution, observe meaningful deviations rather than constantly reconsidering every detail. Afterward, review what happened, identify whether the procedure performed as expected, and distinguish process failures from unusual external events. Revise the procedure only when evidence supports a change, and document lessons that should inform future decisions. Over time, this cycle can improve awareness of recurring conditions and exceptions. Its effectiveness depends on honest feedback, willingness to revise assumptions, and appropriate controls when decisions have significant consequences.

Study instruction:
- Apply a prepare–act–monitor–review cycle to one recurring task for several iterations and document the evidence supporting any process changes.
Key point:
- Commonsense can be strengthened through deliberate practice and reflection.
- Changes to established methods should be linked to evidence and reviewed outcomes.

Table 5
Innovation Through Exceptions, Conflict, and Competitive Learning
1. Q: What is innovation, and how does it differ from ordinary idea generation?
A: Show answer
Innovation is the development of a meaningful new possibility that changes how a problem is understood or how people act. In Chapter 5, Angus Fletcher distinguishes purposeful innovation from ideation, which generates candidate ideas through combinations of existing concepts. A long list of unusual ideas does not automatically produce a breakthrough. Innovation requires recognizing an overlooked possibility, understanding why it matters, and developing it into something that changes an existing practice, product, theory, or system. The chapter identifies three methods for strengthening this process: turn an exception into a new rule, leverage conflict, and eat your enemy by incorporating useful aspects of different thinking. These methods are best understood as a framework for disciplined exploration, not a guarantee that every unusual idea will succeed.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish idea generation, invention, experimentation, implementation, and innovation using an example from science or business.
Key point:
- More ideas do not necessarily produce more meaningful innovation.
- A breakthrough requires a promising insight to be developed and tested.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
2. Q: What is the difference between a grinder, a hacker, and an innovator?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses grinders to represent people who emphasize effort, discipline, and persistence, while hackers represent people who seek efficiencies, shortcuts, and ways to exploit existing systems. Both approaches can be useful, but neither guarantees a breakthrough. Grinding can sustain necessary work without questioning whether the work is worthwhile. Hacking can improve efficiency without changing the underlying problem. An innovator asks whether an overlooked possibility could change the system itself. In practice, successful innovation usually needs effort and efficiency as well as imagination. An organization should therefore avoid treating these categories as fixed personality types. Instead, it should recognize when persistence, process optimization, or a more fundamental rethink is appropriate. The crucial distinction is whether the activity merely improves existing performance or opens a genuinely different path.

Study instruction:
- Classify three workplace improvements as execution, efficiency improvement, or potential innovation, and explain the evidence for each classification.
Key point:
- Hard work and efficiency are valuable but do not replace problem redefinition.
- Innovation questions the assumptions that existing methods depend on.
Source links:
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
3. Q: Why does the chapter treat intuition as a starting point for innovation?
A: Show answer
Intuition can direct attention toward something unusual before a person can fully explain why it matters. An unexpected result, an awkward exception, or a detail that does not fit a familiar pattern may suggest a new question. The chapter argues that creative thinkers can use such observations to imagine possibilities that conventional reasoning initially overlooks. Intuition should not, however, be confused with proof. An intuitive insight can be mistaken, incomplete, or influenced by bias. Its practical value lies in generating a promising line of inquiry that can subsequently be examined, tested, and refined. For example, a scientist may notice that an observation conflicts with a theory, while an entrepreneur may notice an overlooked customer need. In both cases, intuition helps identify where further investigation could be valuable.

Study instruction:
- Record an unexpected observation, the assumption it challenges, and the evidence needed to investigate it.
Key point:
- Intuition can help identify promising questions before formal analysis begins.
- Evidence and testing must determine whether an intuitive explanation is credible.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
4. Q: What is an exception, and why can it become the starting point of a breakthrough?
A: Show answer
An exception is an observation, result, behavior, or case that does not fit an existing expectation. It may reveal a flaw in a rule, an overlooked condition, or an opportunity that the current framework cannot explain. The chapter encourages innovators to investigate exceptions rather than immediately dismiss them as noise. For instance, a product may succeed with a small group of users even though its design does not appeal to the broader market. That unusual success could reveal a neglected need or a distinctive feature worth developing. However, not every exception is significant. It may result from measurement error, coincidence, or an unrepresentative sample. The innovator must determine whether the observation is reliable, repeatable, and meaningful before building a new explanation around it.

Study instruction:
- Identify one exception to a familiar rule and develop at least two competing explanations before deciding whether it signals a new opportunity.
Key point:
- Exceptions deserve investigation because they may expose hidden assumptions.
- An anomaly becomes useful evidence only after its reliability and context are examined.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
5. Q: How does the method “turn an exception into a new rule” work in practice?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying a real anomaly and stating the existing rule it appears to challenge. Next, determine what makes the anomaly different and why the difference might matter. Imagine what would happen if its distinctive feature were available consistently rather than appearing only in an unusual case. Develop a provisional rule that accommodates the exception while explaining the cases covered by the original rule. Finally, test the new rule against observations and alternative explanations. This sequence moves from noticing an anomaly to constructing a hypothesis and evaluating its implications. For example, a service that customers use for an unexpected purpose may suggest a redesign that makes that purpose central. The new rule should not simply declare the exception universally correct; it must explain when, why, and under what conditions the new approach works.

Study instruction:
- Apply the sequence observation, existing rule, exceptional feature, provisional new rule, and validation to a real problem.
Key point:
- A promising exception can inspire a new framework for action.
- The proposed rule must explain evidence and survive testing.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
6. Q: What does Hamlet’s instruction to welcome a stranger teach about innovation?
A: Show answer
In Shakespeare’s Hamlet, Horatio calls an unexpected event strange, and Hamlet responds that it should be welcomed as a stranger. Fletcher uses this exchange as a metaphor for encountering unfamiliar information. Instead of forcing an unusual observation into a familiar category, the thinker should give it enough attention to discover what it may reveal. This does not mean accepting every surprising claim or abandoning standards of evidence. It means resisting the reflex to dismiss something merely because it does not fit current expectations. A practical application is to ask what an unfamiliar customer behavior, scientific result, or colleague’s proposal might reveal before judging it against established procedures. Curiosity allows the observer to investigate the exception; critical evaluation determines what should follow from it.

Study instruction:
- Read the relevant passage in Hamlet and identify how the characters respond to an unfamiliar event.
Key point:
- Curiosity creates room to investigate unfamiliar possibilities.
- Welcoming an anomaly is different from accepting an unsupported conclusion.
Source links:
- Folger Shakespeare Library — Hamlet, Entire Play
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
7. Q: How does the chapter use Albert Einstein to explain innovation through exceptions?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights the constancy of the speed of light as an observation that challenged familiar assumptions about motion and time. Rather than treating the exception as a minor technical problem, Einstein developed a theoretical framework that took the behavior of light seriously. His 1905 paper on the electrodynamics of moving bodies helped establish special relativity, which changed the scientific understanding of space and time. The educational lesson is that an influential theory may emerge when a researcher reorganizes assumptions around an observation that older explanations cannot adequately accommodate. The historical development was more complex than a single moment of insight: it drew on previous scientific work and required mathematical formulation, argument, and subsequent testing. Innovation therefore involves both imaginative reframing and rigorous scientific evaluation.

Study instruction:
- Review Einstein’s 1905 paper and explain how the constancy of light speed relates to the special theory of relativity.
Key point:
- Anomalies can motivate a fundamental revision of a scientific framework.
- Scientific breakthroughs require sustained theoretical and empirical work.
Source links:
- Library of Congress — Einstein’s 1905 Papers
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
8. Q: How can an organization distinguish a valuable exception from random noise?
A: Show answer
A valuable exception has a credible connection to a question worth investigating; random noise may have no stable explanatory significance. The organization should first verify the source, measurement method, and context of the observation. It should then investigate whether the pattern appears again, whether it has a plausible explanation, and whether the explanation predicts anything that can be tested. In business, a sudden increase in demand for one product may reflect a lasting customer need or a temporary promotion. In research, an unexpected result may reveal a genuine effect or an experimental error. Teams should therefore preserve unusual observations while assessing their reliability. Premature dismissal can hide opportunities, while premature acceptance can waste resources and encourage false conclusions.

Study instruction:
- Create a checklist for validating an unusual result, including data quality, context, repeatability, and alternative explanations.
Key point:
- Preserve anomalies for investigation without assuming that they are meaningful.
- Repeatability and explanatory value help distinguish signals from noise.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
9. Q: What is the difference between noticing an exception and developing an innovative theory?
A: Show answer
Noticing an exception is an observational achievement; developing a theory requires explaining it and determining what the explanation implies. A scientist may identify a result that conflicts with prevailing expectations, but several explanations may account for it. A successful theory must organize relevant evidence, clarify the conditions under which its claims apply, and generate consequences that can be assessed. Likewise, a business may observe that customers behave unexpectedly, but it still needs to determine why they behave that way and whether the pattern can support a workable product or service. The chapter’s innovation framework encourages imagination to move beyond merely recording unusual information. Yet imagination must work together with disciplined analysis rather than replace it. The distance between observation and reliable explanation is where much of the intellectual work occurs.

Study instruction:
- Write an observation, two alternative explanations, and one testable prediction for each explanation.
Key point:
- Observation reveals a question; theory proposes an explanation.
- A new explanation gains credibility through evidence and testing.
Source links:
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
- Library of Congress — Einstein’s 1905 Papers
10. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize the unique characteristics of ideas rather than generic improvements?
A: Show answer
Fletcher argues that transformative innovation often develops a distinctive possibility instead of reducing an idea to its most familiar features. A generic improvement may make a product faster, cheaper, or more convenient while leaving its basic conception unchanged. A more fundamental innovation asks what is unusual about the product, user, or context and whether that feature could define a new direction. This does not make incremental improvements worthless; many important advances depend on them. The distinction concerns the scale of the change in assumptions or capabilities. A team should identify what genuinely differentiates its proposed solution and why that characteristic matters to users. It should then determine whether the difference creates value that competing approaches cannot provide as effectively.

Study instruction:
- Compare an incremental improvement with a proposed redesign and identify the assumptions each one changes.
Key point:
- Distinctiveness can reveal opportunities for a new product or approach.
- Incremental and transformative innovations both have legitimate roles.
Source links:
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
- Harvard Business Review — How Constructive Dissent Can Unlock Your Team’s Innovation
11. Q: What does Steve Jobs’s experience with the ROKR phone illustrate about innovation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the Motorola ROKR, a phone developed with Apple technology that offered limited access to a music library and performed poorly in the market. Rather than interpreting the disappointing result only as evidence that music and phones were an unworkable combination, Jobs is portrayed as recognizing the larger possibility of integrating music into a dedicated mobile device. The lesson is to examine failure at a more precise level: Which part failed, which underlying need remains promising, and what could be redesigned? This approach does not imply that a failed product should always be continued. Teams should distinguish a weak implementation from a weak underlying proposition and use evidence to decide whether a redesign is justified. A failure may contain useful information, but only careful diagnosis reveals it.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a failed product and separate the customer need, core concept, implementation defects, and evidence required for a redesign.
Key point:
- A failed implementation does not automatically invalidate the underlying idea.
- Further investment requires evidence that a better design can solve the identified problems.
Source links:
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
12. Q: What does the chapter mean by “singularity” in creative thinking?
A: Show answer
Singularity means attending to the particular features that make a person, object, idea, or situation distinctive. Fletcher contrasts this with an approach that quickly classifies things under broad categories or archetypes. General categories can help people communicate and compare, but they may hide characteristics that make a particular case valuable. In literature, a character may be more interesting than a familiar personality label suggests. In technology, a device may have an unusual feature that becomes the foundation for a new product category. Creative thinking requires moving between specific details and general concepts: the observer identifies what is distinctive, then develops a broader explanation or application that remains faithful to the evidence. Singular features are not automatically valuable, but they may be worth exploring before they are generalized away.

Study instruction:
- Describe a product or character without using generic labels, then identify which specific characteristics make it distinctive.
Key point:
- Specific differences can reveal possibilities hidden by broad categories.
- Abstraction is useful, but it should not erase evidence that matters.
Source links:
- Folger Shakespeare Library — Hamlet, Entire Play
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
13. Q: How can curiosity help a person notice possibilities that experienced professionals overlook?
A: Show answer
Experience provides patterns that help people recognize situations quickly, but those patterns can also encourage them to overlook unfamiliar details. Curiosity interrupts automatic classification by prompting questions about what is different, surprising, or unexplained. The chapter’s examples suggest that creative thinkers engage with works, objects, and observations outside the narrow purpose for which they are normally studied. A professional may notice a new application by experimenting with a tool rather than merely following its established use. This does not mean ignoring expertise; curiosity and expertise are complementary. Expertise helps the observer understand what is unusual, while curiosity prevents familiar explanations from ending the investigation too early. Productive curiosity must ultimately lead to more accurate understanding, useful experiments, or a clearer question.

Study instruction:
- Examine a familiar tool or process and list three unexpected applications, then assess the feasibility of each.
Key point:
- Curiosity helps experts question assumptions built through experience.
- Unusual possibilities need evaluation before they can become practical solutions.
Source links:
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
- Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Scientific Discovery
14. Q: What is the “reality-distortion field,” and what lesson should leaders draw from it?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the term “reality-distortion field” to describe Jobs’s ability to challenge ordinary expectations and persuade others to pursue an ambitious vision. As a metaphor for innovation, it represents the temporary suspension of assumptions that make a new possibility seem impossible. Such ambition can help a team focus on potential rather than accept current limitations automatically. However, persuasive confidence can also cause people to ignore costs, risks, and contradictory evidence. Leaders should therefore combine ambitious goals with transparent assumptions, realistic resource plans, independent review, and explicit conditions for stopping or changing direction. The useful lesson is not to make people believe anything, but to help them investigate possibilities that existing expectations may be prematurely excluding.

Study instruction:
- Develop a bold project proposal and record its assumptions, resource requirements, evidence thresholds, and failure conditions.
Key point:
- Vision can expand what a team considers possible.
- Confidence must not replace evidence, accountability, or risk management.
Source links:
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
- Harvard Business Review — How to Preempt Team Conflict
15. Q: Why does the chapter criticize ideation based on random combinations, and does that mean brainstorming is useless?
A: Show answer
The chapter criticizes ideation when a high volume of combinations is mistaken for genuine innovation. Random association can produce possibilities, but it may not identify the exceptional observation, customer need, or explanatory insight that makes one possibility important. This criticism should not be interpreted as proof that brainstorming or divergent thinking has no value. Exploring alternatives can widen the solution space and help teams avoid premature commitment. The important distinction is between generating candidates and developing a solution that addresses a real problem. A disciplined process begins with understanding the challenge, explores multiple directions, tests promising options, and refines the results. Ideation is one tool within that process, not a substitute for observation, judgment, experimentation, and implementation.

Study instruction:
- Run a brainstorming exercise, then evaluate the proposals using problem relevance, supporting evidence, feasibility, and expected impact.
Key point:
- Idea quantity and idea quality are different measures.
- Divergent thinking is most useful when paired with evaluation and testing.
Source links:
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
16. Q: What does it mean to leverage conflict instead of eliminating it?
A: Show answer
The second innovation method treats conflict between an established rule and an exceptional case as an opportunity to develop a better framework. Instead of quickly choosing one side or accepting a superficial compromise, the thinker investigates what each side reveals. The established rule may serve an important purpose, while the exception may expose a neglected possibility. A new rule can sometimes preserve the original purpose while accommodating the new possibility. In an organization, for example, a standard approval process may protect accountability but slow urgent field operations. Rather than eliminating approval controls or accepting every delay, a team can develop risk-based authorization thresholds and retrospective review for defined exceptions. Constructive conflict is therefore a method for re-examining assumptions while retaining legitimate objectives and safeguards.

Study instruction:
- Select a conflict between a standard procedure and an exceptional need, and formulate a solution that preserves the purpose of both.
Key point:
- Conflict can reveal limitations in the way a problem is framed.
- A redesigned rule should preserve valid objectives while addressing the exception.
Source links:
- Harvard Business Review — How Constructive Dissent Can Unlock Your Team’s Innovation
- Harvard Business Review — How to Preempt Team Conflict
17. Q: Why can resolving a disagreement too quickly undermine innovation?
A: Show answer
A quick decision may preserve harmony or save time, but it can also prevent a team from understanding why its members disagree. One person may be protecting a valuable principle while another has noticed a condition the principle does not handle well. If the group immediately chooses a winner or compromises without examining the underlying reasons, it may lose the insight that could improve the whole approach. The chapter recommends sustaining the tension long enough to imagine a new possibility. This does not mean prolonging every argument. Teams should use time limits, clear decision criteria, and explicit responsibilities to ensure that productive disagreement leads to action. The objective is to extract useful information from competing views, not to make indecision a permanent operating style.

Study instruction:
- Review a past team decision and identify whether disagreement was examined, suppressed, or resolved through an evidence-based decision process.
Key point:
- Premature agreement can conceal important assumptions and risks.
- Productive disagreement needs a defined route to a decision.
Source links:
- Harvard Business Review Research — Putting Your Company’s Whole Brain to Work
- Harvard Business Review — How to Preempt Team Conflict
18. Q: How can a team create a new rule that combines the “why” of an existing rule with the “what if” of an exception?
A: Show answer
First, identify the purpose the current rule is intended to serve. Then identify the unusual need or possibility that the rule fails to accommodate. Ask what would happen if the exceptional condition were accepted without abandoning the purpose of the existing rule. Develop alternative designs that could satisfy both, assess their risks, and test whether they work. For instance, a rule requiring advance approval may exist to prevent unauthorized spending. An urgent field situation may require rapid action. A possible redesigned procedure could allow small, predefined emergency purchases within an approved limit, followed by documentation and independent review. The rule’s original purpose is preserved while its application becomes more adaptable. The final design should be documented and approved by the appropriate authority.

Study instruction:
- Write the purpose, exception, proposed new rule, control safeguards, and test criteria for one organizational procedure.
Key point:
- The purpose of a rule may be retained even when its original form changes.
- Revised procedures must be tested, approved, and monitored.
Source links:
- Harvard Business Review — How Constructive Dissent Can Unlock Your Team’s Innovation
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
19. Q: What does the Delilah example illustrate about balancing individual needs and community responsibilities?
A: Show answer
In the chapter’s illustrative story, Delilah does not want to follow her community’s expectation that young people should marry at eighteen. Instead of simply accepting the rule or rejecting it, she examines its underlying purpose: encouraging young people to learn to care for others rather than remain solely focused on themselves. She proposes studying veterinary science and returning to serve her farming community. The new arrangement allows her to pursue her distinctive interests while meeting the community’s broader objective. The example demonstrates how a conflict may be resolved by separating a rule’s purpose from its conventional form. In real organizations, such a solution would also need consideration of fairness, individual rights, practical feasibility, and the consent of affected people. A compelling story illustrates a method; it does not, by itself, prove that a proposed policy will work everywhere.

Study instruction:
- Identify the rule, the personal exception, the rule’s underlying purpose, and the alternative arrangement in the Delilah scenario.
Key point:
- Reframing a conflict can reveal more than two apparent choices.
- Solutions must account for the rights and circumstances of affected people.

- Harvard Business Review — How Constructive Dissent Can Unlock Your Team’s Innovation
- Design Council — Framework for Innovation
20. Q: What does the Special Operations obstacle-course example teach about designing rules around objectives?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a recruit who reaches a required bell by going around an obstacle course rather than completing the prescribed route. The trainers must decide whether the purpose of the test is to navigate the designated obstacles or to reach a target within a time limit. The example illustrates how an apparent violation can expose ambiguity between a procedure and its intended objective. If the objective is independent navigation, a more effective assessment may allow different routes while retaining time, safety, and destination requirements. In practice, organizations must distinguish legitimate flexibility from behavior that defeats a control’s purpose. A payment procedure, for example, may allow an emergency route only when specified authorization and documentation safeguards are satisfied. Rules should be designed around clear outcomes without inviting uncontrolled workarounds.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite a rigid procedure as an objective-based test and specify the safeguards that cannot be bypassed.
Key point:
- A procedure and its underlying objective are not always identical.
- Flexibility must not undermine safety, authorization, or accountability.
