24. Q: What is the difference between uncertainty, volatility, and novelty?
A: Show answer
Uncertainty concerns the limits of what is known about an outcome, its causes, or the information needed to choose well. Volatility describes the degree or speed of change in relevant conditions or outcomes. Novelty concerns how far a situation differs from familiar patterns and established experience. These concepts can overlap but are not identical. A familiar process may be volatile because demand fluctuates predictably, while a novel event may be difficult to classify even before measurable changes become large. Decision-makers should determine which problem they face before choosing a response. Volatility may require faster monitoring, uncertainty may require additional evidence, and novelty may require new hypotheses or approaches. The distinctions help avoid treating every difficulty as the same type of risk.

Study instruction:
- Classify three hypothetical situations by uncertainty, volatility, and novelty, allowing more than one category where appropriate.

Key point:
- Different kinds of change require different diagnostic responses.
- Do not confuse uncertainty with the speed or magnitude of change.

Source links:
- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
25. Q: How should a decision-maker match the novelty of a plan to the novelty of the situation?
A: Show answer
Begin by determining whether the present conditions are sufficiently similar to those under which the existing plan worked. If they are, the familiar method may remain appropriate. If important assumptions have weakened, examine whether modifying the current plan is sufficient. If the situation is fundamentally different, develop an alternative that addresses the new conditions rather than forcing the old plan to fit. The level of experimentation should reflect the seriousness of the change, the evidence available, and the consequences of error. Maintain clear decision criteria and monitor results so that the approach can be revised again if necessary. The objective is neither automatic continuity nor automatic disruption, but a response suited to the actual problem.

Study instruction:
- Apply the framework to a workplace process and document the evidence for retaining, modifying, or replacing its current plan.

Key point:
- Familiar conditions support familiar plans.
- Fundamentally different conditions may require fundamentally different responses.

Source links:
- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
26. Q: Why do people sometimes take unnecessary risks in stable situations but hesitate during genuine disruption?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that prolonged stability can reduce awareness of risk because familiar routines make the environment feel predictable. In such conditions, people may underestimate uncertainty or spend resources on unnecessary experiments. During disruption, the opposite can occur: anxiety, incomplete information, and fear of making a mistake may cause decision-makers to delay action. Both responses reflect a mismatch between decision behavior and the actual conditions. Better judgment begins by assessing the situation independently of emotional comfort. Stable conditions call for disciplined attention to priorities, while disruption calls for structured reassessment and proportionate action. Neither comfort nor fear is sufficient evidence that a proposed decision is appropriate.

Study instruction:
- Identify a situation in which emotional comfort or anxiety could distort the assessment of risk and specify an evidence-based corrective step.

Key point:
- Subjective confidence does not reliably measure objective risk.
- Decisions should reflect the situation, not emotional comfort alone.

Source links:
- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
27. Q: How can anxiety help identify the need to reconsider a decision?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects situational awareness with noticing when circumstances become more volatile or less predictable. Anxiety can sometimes signal that expectations and observations no longer align, but it is not a reliable measurement of danger by itself. A person may feel anxious in a safe situation or remain calm while overlooking a serious risk. Therefore, emotional signals should initiate assessment rather than determine the conclusion. The decision-maker should identify what has changed, distinguish observable evidence from interpretation, and review the assumptions underlying the current plan. If the evidence confirms rising uncertainty, it may be appropriate to develop alternatives or seek additional expertise. This approach treats anxiety as a potential prompt for investigation rather than as an instruction to panic or withdraw.

Study instruction:
- Write down one concern, the observable evidence supporting it, alternative explanations, and the action needed to resolve uncertainty.

Key point:
- Emotional signals may prompt useful investigation.
- Decisions require evidence, context, and proportionate judgment.

- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
28. Q: How can teams develop alternative plans before a routine process becomes a crisis?
A: Show answer
Teams can monitor early signs of declining effectiveness and use them to begin developing alternatives while the existing process still functions. This creates time to investigate causes, test ideas, consult specialists, and define safe transition arrangements. Contingency planning should identify which assumptions may fail, what indicators would reveal the failure, who has authority to act, and which alternatives are available. Plans should also define decision triggers and resource limits so that a response can be initiated without excessive delay. Preparing alternatives does not mean constantly abandoning the current method. It means preserving options and reducing dependence on a single approach when evidence suggests that conditions may change.

Study instruction:
- Create a contingency plan for one recurring process, including early-warning indicators, decision triggers, owners, and fallback options.

Key point:
- Contingency planning preserves options before urgency increases.
- Early preparation reduces the cost of adapting later.

29. Q: What does the chapter's ambush example illustrate about initiative and decision-making?
A: Show answer
The ambush story illustrates the broader idea that a sudden change can remove the advantage of following an ordinary plan. The chapter uses the example to discuss recovering initiative, disrupting the assumption that events will continue as expected, and avoiding passive dependence on a memorized response. Its central lesson for general decision-making is that people should not confuse a familiar script with a complete understanding of the situation. The example is not a universal tactical instruction; real emergencies require trained judgment, context-specific procedures, and appropriate safety guidance. In ordinary organizations, the analogous response is to recognize the disruption, establish priorities, assess available options, and act within one's authority rather than waiting indefinitely for perfect information.

Key point:
- Sudden disruption can invalidate familiar response patterns.
- Initiative should operate within context and safety constraints.

30. Q: Why is restoring initiative important when an unexpected event disrupts a plan?
A: Show answer
Initiative allows a person or team to influence events rather than merely reacting to every new development. When an unexpected disruption occurs, a purely reactive approach can lead to repeated delays, fragmented decisions, and dependence on other people to define the next step. Restoring initiative begins with identifying the most important objective, establishing what is currently known, and taking an appropriate action that improves the situation or produces useful information. In some cases, that means pausing unsafe activity or escalating the problem rather than acting immediately. Effective initiative is purposeful and informed. Its value lies in restoring agency and creating better options, not in maximizing activity or making unsupported decisions.

Study instruction:
- Describe a disrupted project and identify the smallest authorized action that would clarify the situation or restore progress.

Key point:
- Initiative creates options under changing conditions.
- Useful action must remain aligned with objectives and constraints.

31. Q: What can George Marshall's leadership example teach about managing expertise during changing conditions?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses Marshall to illustrate the need to prepare for fundamentally different circumstances instead of treating previous success as sufficient proof of future readiness. His historical role as U.S. Army chief of staff included mobilization, organizational preparation, staff planning, and adaptation to the demands of World War II. The broader lesson is that organizations must periodically question whether their people, structures, and established methods remain suitable for their current challenges. Seniority and past achievement should not prevent necessary reassessment. At the same time, changes in personnel or strategy should be based on documented capability needs and fair evaluation rather than on a preference for novelty alone. Leadership requires balancing respect for experience with readiness to change.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate an organization facing a major change and identify the skills, processes, or roles that need reassessment.

Key point:
- Organizational readiness must evolve with operating conditions.
- Experience matters, but it does not remove the need for reassessment.

32. Q: How does Thomas Paine's Common Sense illustrate the relationship between an incomplete idea and a major change?
A: Show answer
Paine's pamphlet Common Sense, published in 1776, argued for American independence and helped shape public debate about the colonies' political future. The chapter uses his example to illustrate how a new direction may become influential before all practical details have been settled. The relevant lesson is that an unfamiliar proposal can create a framework for further thinking and action. However, an inspiring argument is not, by itself, proof that every aspect of a proposed change will succeed. Effective decision-makers must evaluate the idea's substance, consider its implications, and develop workable methods of implementation. Paine's example illustrates the importance of articulating a different possibility when established arrangements no longer appear sufficient.

Study instruction:
- Examine a major reform proposal and distinguish the central argument from the operational details required to implement it.

- A new direction may begin with a compelling but incomplete proposal.
- Vision must be followed by practical assessment and execution.

33. Q: What does George Washington's decision-making illustrate about matching a plan to the circumstances?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents Washington as a leader who used different approaches in different circumstances rather than applying the same style to every problem. The siege of Yorktown and the winter crossing of the Delaware are used as contrasting examples of decisions made under different strategic conditions. The broader lesson is that a decision-maker should evaluate the objective, available resources, uncertainty, and consequences before choosing a course of action. A routine task may justify a familiar procedure, while a severe disruption may require an alternative. However, the presence of uncertainty alone does not guarantee that a risky action will succeed. Decision-makers should also evaluate feasibility, the likely consequences of failure, and whether the proposed action is proportionate to the objective.

Study instruction:
- Compare two contrasting historical decisions and identify how objectives, resources, uncertainty, and consequences shaped their approaches.

Key point:
- Effective leaders adapt their methods to the circumstances.
- Context-sensitive judgment is more useful than a single universal style.

34. Q: How does Neil Armstrong's Gemini VIII emergency illustrate adaptive decision-making?
A: Show answer
During Gemini VIII in March 1966, a spacecraft malfunction caused a dangerous tumbling motion after docking. Armstrong and David Scott worked to regain control, and Armstrong used the reentry control system to stabilize the spacecraft after disabling the malfunctioning attitude-control system. NASA's mission history documents the emergency response and the decision to terminate the mission early. The episode illustrates the importance of recognizing when normal operations are no longer safe and taking action consistent with the actual technical problem. It does not imply that improvisation should replace training. Armstrong's response depended on technical knowledge, mission preparation, and interpreting an unusual situation while managing the consequences of continued operation.

Study instruction:
- Review the NASA mission history and identify the malfunction, the evidence available, the response, and the safety trade-off involved.

- An unforeseen failure may require departure from normal operations.

35. Q: What did Armstrong's Lunar Landing Research Vehicle accident reveal about trusting instruments and established plans?
A: Show answer
On May 6, 1968, Armstrong ejected from a Lunar Landing Research Vehicle after it became uncontrollable during a training flight. NASA's account explains that a loss of helium pressure depleted the hydrogen peroxide used by the reserve attitude thrusters and that the instrumentation did not provide adequate warning of the danger. The incident illustrates why instruments and established procedures must be understood within their limitations. It does not justify ignoring reliable instruments whenever intuition disagrees. Instead, a mismatch between observed behavior, system indications, and expected performance should trigger a disciplined safety assessment. This may involve following emergency procedures, consulting other evidence, and taking the authorized action necessary to protect life.

Study instruction:
- Read NASA's accident account and identify the equipment failure, limitations of the warning system, and role of the pilot's judgment.

Key point:
- System indicators can be incomplete or misleading under failure conditions.
- Conflicting evidence requires disciplined assessment and safety action.

36. Q: How did the Apollo 11 lunar landing demonstrate the value of combining automation and human judgment?
A: Show answer
During the Apollo 11 descent, the lunar module guidance system supported the landing, but Armstrong encountered rugged terrain near the intended landing area. The mission record describes his switch to manual control and selection of a safer landing location. This example illustrates a complementary relationship: automation can perform calculations and execute defined operations, while a human operator may need to respond to contextual features that challenge the planned route. Effective use of automation requires clear responsibilities, adequate situational awareness, and a safe way to intervene when necessary. Human intervention should be trained and supported by system design rather than improvised without preparation. The example is especially relevant where the physical environment can differ from the simplified conditions represented in a model.

Study instruction:
- Review NASA's Apollo 11 mission summary and explain why the actual landing environment required a change to the planned approach.

Key point:
- Automation and human judgment can complement each other.
- Safe intervention depends on training, awareness, and control design.

37. Q: What is the difference between a useful simulation and an adequate substitute for real-world judgment?
A: Show answer
A simulation provides a controlled environment for practicing defined tasks and exploring selected failures. Its value depends on how accurately it represents the conditions relevant to performance. No simulation can automatically guarantee that every real-world event has been represented. Its assumptions, omissions, and operating boundaries therefore need to be understood. A strong training program combines simulation with analysis of unexpected events, practical experience where safe, and explicit instruction on when ordinary procedures or model outputs require reassessment. The objective is to build confidence without creating false certainty. Participants should learn not only to perform well within the simulation but also to recognize when a real situation differs in important ways from the scenarios used for practice.

Study instruction:
- Identify three assumptions in a simulation and explain how the real environment could violate each one.

Key point:
- Simulations are valuable but represent only selected conditions.
- Learners must understand the boundaries of simulated experience.

38. Q: How does the optimization trap apply to artificial intelligence systems?
A: Show answer
An AI system can perform very well on data and tasks similar to those used during its development while producing unreliable results in different conditions. The chapter uses this possibility to illustrate how speed and consistency can magnify mistakes when a system's assumptions no longer fit its operating environment. The practical response is to evaluate the system under realistic conditions, monitor performance after deployment, document limitations, and establish procedures for handling unexpected outputs. AI should not be assumed safe merely because its historical accuracy is high. Nor does the possibility of failure mean that all AI systems are unsuitable. Appropriate use depends on the task, the consequences of error, the quality of evidence, and the safeguards surrounding the system.

Study instruction:
- Identify the operating assumptions of an AI application and specify what monitoring and escalation should occur when its performance changes.

Key point:
- Strong historical performance does not guarantee reliable future behavior.
- AI risk management must continue throughout the system's use.

39. Q: How can an AI system's performance deteriorate when real-world data changes?
A: Show answer
Performance may deteriorate when incoming data no longer resemble the conditions represented in training or testing. Changes in user behavior, operating environments, measurement methods, or underlying relationships can make earlier patterns less reliable. This is one reason model performance should be evaluated beyond a single initial test. Teams should define relevant indicators, monitor input and output characteristics, investigate anomalies, and establish criteria for retraining, restricting, or replacing a system when justified. Human review is particularly important where errors could cause significant harm. Not every change requires replacing the model; some changes may be addressed through better data, revised workflows, or clearer limits on use. The response should follow evidence and the consequences of failure.

Study instruction:
- Design a monitoring plan that specifies performance indicators, review frequency, investigation thresholds, and escalation responsibilities for an AI tool.

Key point:
- Changing data can weaken the validity of earlier model assessments.
- Monitoring must connect detected problems to defined actions.

40. Q: What roles should humans retain when AI supports consequential decisions?
A: Show answer
Humans should retain clearly defined responsibility for the decisions assigned to them, especially where outputs have material consequences or require context that the system may not represent. The required level of human involvement depends on the application, the risk of error, the reliability of the system, and the applicable rules. Human review must be meaningful: reviewers need sufficient information, time, competence, and authority to question outputs or stop the process when necessary. Simply placing a person at the end of an automated workflow does not guarantee effective oversight. Organizations should also monitor whether users become overly dependent on recommendations and whether the interface encourages independent assessment. The objective is a well-designed partnership between automated analysis and accountable human judgment.

Study instruction:
- Map an AI-supported decision process and specify who owns the decision, who checks the result, and when the process must stop for further review.

Key point:
- Human oversight requires competence, authority, and meaningful review.
- Accountability should be explicit rather than assumed.

41. Q: How should organizations introduce automation without becoming dependent on it?
A: Show answer
Organizations should use automation to improve repeatable tasks while preserving the ability to identify and handle exceptions. This requires clear operating limits, reliable data, documented assumptions, defined ownership, and a review process for unexpected results. Staff should understand what the automated system does, what it does not do, and how to escalate problems. Important workflows should have tested alternatives for outages or failures, proportionate to their criticality. Organizations should also monitor whether users can still recognize errors instead of accepting system outputs automatically. The aim is not to avoid automation but to make it dependable within a defined purpose while retaining the human and organizational capacity to respond when that purpose or environment changes.

Study instruction:
- Review an automated workflow and document its limitations, exception controls, manual fallback, and conditions for suspension.

Key point:
- Automation should reduce routine effort without eliminating exception capability.
- Clear ownership and fallback arrangements support resilience.

42. Q: How can a team use an expert's advice without surrendering its own judgment?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends consulting an expert to determine whether a proposed plan contains a fatal flaw rather than automatically asking the expert to supply the entire solution. This approach can help a team benefit from specialist knowledge while retaining responsibility for its own context-specific choices. A practical review should identify the objective, evidence, assumptions, likely failure modes, and conditions under which the plan would be unacceptable. The expert should be asked to explain any demonstrated failure mechanism and the evidence supporting it. The team must still consider legal requirements, safety, resources, and its own operational knowledge. An expert's inability to identify a decisive flaw does not prove that the plan will succeed; it merely means that one particular objection has not been established.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a proposed plan and ask a knowledgeable reviewer to identify specific conditions under which it would fail.

Key point:
- Experts can help identify weaknesses without owning every decision.
- Failure to prove a flaw is not proof of success.

- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
43. Q: What does “Go Where Experts Can't Say No” mean as a decision-making technique?
A: Show answer
In the chapter, the technique asks an expert to assess whether a proposed plan can be shown to fail. The intention is to identify fatal weaknesses without automatically allowing conventional preferences to eliminate every unfamiliar option. Used carefully, it can reveal whether criticism rests on evidence or merely on a preference for familiar approaches. However, the technique has limits. An expert may lack relevant information, a risk may be difficult to prove in advance, and the absence of a demonstrated failure does not establish safety, legality, or success. The method should therefore complement ordinary risk assessment rather than replace it. Decision-makers must still test critical assumptions, consider plausible harms, apply required safeguards, and recognize when further evidence is necessary.

Study instruction:
- Apply the technique to a low-risk proposal and record both demonstrated failure mechanisms and unresolved uncertainties.

Key point:
- A focused challenge can expose weaknesses in an unfamiliar plan.
- Passing a challenge does not establish that a plan is safe or successful.

44. Q: How can a team test a proposed plan before committing significant resources?
A: Show answer
A team should translate the plan into testable assumptions, define the outcomes that would indicate success or failure, and evaluate the most important risks. Where practical, use a limited pilot, simulation, prototype, or reversible experiment to gather evidence at manageable cost. Independent review can help expose assumptions that the proposal's creators overlook. Testing should include expected conditions and plausible adverse scenarios, not only the easiest case. The team should determine in advance which results would justify continuing, revising, or stopping the plan. High-risk or irreversible decisions may require formal approval and more extensive evidence before action. This process preserves room for innovation while reducing the likelihood that enthusiasm will substitute for validation.

Study instruction:
- Design a pilot with a hypothesis, success criteria, failure thresholds, monitoring arrangements, and an explicit decision rule for continuation.

Key point:
- Small tests can reduce uncertainty before large commitments.
- Define decision criteria before interpreting the results.

45. Q: How should decision-makers respond when current performance declines but there is not yet conclusive evidence of failure?
A: Show answer
Declining performance should trigger structured investigation rather than an immediate assumption that the entire plan is obsolete. First, confirm that the measurements are reliable and comparable across periods. Next, determine whether the decline reflects execution problems, temporary fluctuations, resource constraints, or a genuine change in the operating environment. Examine the assumptions supporting the current plan and identify which new evidence would distinguish among these explanations. If necessary, test limited modifications or develop an alternative while maintaining appropriate controls. The decision-maker should document uncertainty and establish when the assessment will be revisited. This approach avoids both premature abandonment of a useful method and prolonged dependence on one that is becoming unsuitable.

Study instruction:
- Investigate a hypothetical decline in performance and develop competing explanations, tests, and review thresholds.

Key point:
- Weakening results require investigation, not automatic replacement.
- Decisions should respond to evidence and the trend's significance.

46. Q: How can an organization avoid confusing stubbornness with disciplined persistence?
A: Show answer
Disciplined persistence means continuing a plan because current evidence supports it and the underlying assumptions remain valid. Stubbornness means continuing mainly because the organization has already invested time, money, or reputation in the plan. To distinguish the two, define the intended outcomes, measure actual results, identify conditions that would invalidate the approach, and review performance against those criteria. Decision-makers should be willing to modify or stop a plan when evidence crosses established thresholds. They should also avoid abandoning a strategy simply because progress is temporarily difficult. A documented review process reduces the influence of sunk costs, personal attachment, and pressure to defend previous decisions. Persistence becomes productive when it remains accountable to the objective and current evidence.

Study instruction:
- Create explicit continuation, modification, and termination criteria for an ongoing initiative.

Key point:
- Persistence is justified by evidence, not by past investment alone.
- Predefined review criteria make course corrections easier to defend.

- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
47. Q: How can leaders communicate a change of plan without creating confusion or undermining trust?
A: Show answer
Leaders should explain what changed, which previous assumptions are no longer reliable, what evidence supports the revised approach, and what the organization must do next. They should distinguish confirmed facts from remaining uncertainties and state which decisions are final versus provisional. Employees also need clear information about responsibilities, timing, constraints, and how emerging problems should be reported. A change of plan should not be presented as proof that all previous work was useless; the earlier approach may have been appropriate under earlier conditions. Documenting the reasoning helps people understand the change and apply the same logic to future decisions. Communication should be timely enough to support action without overstating the certainty of the available evidence.

Study instruction:
- Draft a short change announcement that identifies the trigger, evidence, revised objective, responsibilities, and review point.

Key point:
- Explain the evidence and assumptions behind a change.
- Clear responsibilities reduce implementation errors.

48. Q: What is the role of initiative and adaptability in a team working under uncertainty?
A: Show answer
Initiative allows team members to identify problems and take appropriate action without waiting for every detail to be dictated centrally. Adaptability enables them to revise methods when conditions change. These capabilities work best when the team understands its purpose, authority, constraints, and escalation requirements. Without clear boundaries, initiative can become fragmented or unsafe; without initiative, a team may wait too long for instructions while circumstances deteriorate. Leaders should communicate the desired outcome, identify decisions that can be made locally, and define situations that require approval or immediate escalation. After an unexpected event, the team should review what happened and improve the process. The aim is coordinated action supported by shared understanding, not unrestricted individual discretion.

Study instruction:
- Define which decisions a team member may make independently and which require consultation, approval, or escalation.

Key point:
- Initiative works best with a clear purpose and defined authority.
- Adaptability must remain coordinated with team objectives and safeguards.

49. Q: How can the adaptive decision-making framework be applied to a business facing sudden market change?
A: Show answer
Suppose a product remains profitable but customer complaints increase, repeat purchases weaken, and competitors introduce a new service model. The business should not assume that its existing strategy remains suitable simply because historical sales were strong. It should verify the indicators, gather current customer evidence, and determine whether the problem is operational or strategic. The team can then compare several responses, including improving the existing product, conducting a limited pilot of a new offering, or changing distribution. Each option should have defined objectives, costs, risks, and stopping criteria. The business can retain stable procedures for routine operations while testing new approaches where the evidence indicates that the current model is losing effectiveness.

Study instruction:
- Develop three response options for this scenario and compare their evidence requirements, costs, risks, and review criteria.

Key point:
- Declining effectiveness may indicate a need for strategic adaptation.
- Limited experiments can test alternatives before full implementation.

50. Q: How can adaptive decision-making be evaluated after an important decision has been made?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should examine both the outcome and the quality of the decision process. A good decision can produce an unfavorable outcome because of events that could not reasonably have been predicted. A poor decision can occasionally produce a favorable outcome by chance. Reviewers should therefore assess what information was available at the time, whether assumptions were reasonable, how risks were considered, whether warning signals were recognized, and how the team responded to new evidence. Compare predicted and actual results, identify unexpected developments, and distinguish execution errors from limitations in the original plan. Lessons should be recorded and translated into improved procedures or training. The aim is not to justify every outcome but to improve future judgment through evidence-based learning.

Study instruction:
- Conduct a post-decision review that separates outcome quality, decision quality, execution, unexpected events, and lessons for future action.

Key point:
- Evaluate decisions using the evidence available when they were made.
- Learning requires distinguishing process failures from unpredictable outcomes.

Source links:
- Primal Intelligence — Penguin Random House
- AI Risk Management Framework — NIST
51. Q: How can individuals develop a regular practice of adaptive decision-making?
A: Show answer
Individuals can develop this capacity by deliberately reviewing their assumptions, monitoring the effectiveness of their current methods, and practicing alternative ways of approaching a problem. Before making a decision, identify the objective, the evidence available, the key uncertainties, and the consequences of error. After acting, compare expected results with actual outcomes and consider whether new information changes the assessment. When circumstances are unfamiliar, seek targeted feedback, test assumptions where feasible, and avoid treating established habits as unquestionable rules. It is also useful to learn from people with different experience and to rehearse situations that require a change of approach. Over time, this routine can strengthen the ability to balance disciplined execution with timely adaptation.

Study instruction:
- Keep a weekly decision journal recording assumptions, expected outcomes, uncertainties, actual results, and lessons learned.

Key point:
- Adaptive judgment develops through deliberate practice and reflection.
- Repeatedly testing assumptions improves future decisions.
