27. Q: What does the chapter mean by the limits of optimization?
A: Show answer
Fletcher argues that optimization is particularly effective when objectives, constraints, and available alternatives are sufficiently well defined. It can help select a more efficient solution within an established framework. His concern is that improving a known process is not always the same as inventing an entirely new approach. A system designed to improve existing measures may repeatedly favor solutions that resemble previous successes, leaving unusual possibilities unexplored. The distinction matters in business, science, education, and technology, where efficiency and invention serve related but different purposes. Yet optimization does not categorically prevent innovation. Search methods, simulations, and optimization algorithms can be components of creative discovery. A more precise question is whether the objective function and search space allow the system to discover alternatives that were not previously considered, and whether the chosen measures adequately reflect the desired outcome.

Study instruction:
- Choose a process that is already being optimized and identify what its current metrics reward, what they omit, and how the problem could be reframed.
Key point:
- Optimization improves performance against specified objectives.
- Innovation may require challenging the objectives or assumptions themselves.
Source links:
- Divergent Thinking — Cambridge Handbook of Creativity
28. Q: How does the chapter explain the influence of computers on modern approaches to creativity?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes how computational approaches encouraged people to represent more activities in numerical terms and to use measurable outcomes to improve performance. He connects this development with optimization, analytics, and systematic idea generation. The advantage of these approaches is that they can process large amounts of information, compare alternatives, identify patterns, and test clearly defined objectives. Their limitations depend on how the problem is framed, the quality of the data, the model’s assumptions, and whether important outcomes can be measured. Fletcher worries that treating computation as a complete theory of intelligence encourages people to neglect imagination and attention to exceptions. In practice, computational methods and human judgment can be combined: data can reveal patterns, while contextual reasoning helps determine whether the patterns matter and whether the task itself needs to be redefined.

Study instruction:
- Identify one organizational decision currently driven by metrics and explain how contextual evidence or an unmeasured factor could change the decision.
Key point:
- Computation can improve analysis of measurable patterns.
- Metrics cannot replace scrutiny of assumptions and objectives.
Source links:
- Divergent Thinking — Cambridge Handbook of Creativity
29. Q: What role does J. P. Guilford play in the chapter’s account of creativity?
A: Show answer
Fletcher discusses psychologist J. P. Guilford as an influential figure in twentieth-century creativity research. Guilford’s work helped establish divergent thinking as an important concept in the study of creative potential. Fletcher presents the combination of divergent and convergent thinking as a model for generating and refining ideas: first produce multiple possibilities, then evaluate them against relevant criteria. He uses this history to explain how an approach to creativity became compatible with systematic ideation and computational methods. The historical account should not be read as implying that all modern creativity research accepts one complete two-step model. Creativity research includes varied definitions, measures, and theories, and performance on divergent-thinking tasks captures only part of creative ability. Guilford’s contribution is therefore best understood as an important development in a broader research field rather than the final explanation of how innovation occurs.

Study instruction:
- Read Guilford’s 1950 paper and identify how its discussion of creativity relates to the chapter’s account of ideation.
Key point:
- Guilford was influential in the development of modern creativity research.
- Divergent thinking measures one aspect of creativity, not the entirety of creative achievement.
Source links:
- Creativity — J. P. Guilford, American Psychologist
30. Q: What are divergent thinking and convergent thinking?
A: Show answer
Divergent thinking involves generating multiple possible responses to a question or problem. Convergent thinking involves evaluating possibilities and identifying a solution that best meets relevant requirements. For example, a team might first generate several ways to improve a customer service process and then compare the options according to cost, feasibility, accessibility, and expected benefits. Fletcher discusses this pairing as part of the historical development of ideation. A useful implementation keeps the stages distinct enough to avoid prematurely rejecting unfamiliar ideas, while still ensuring that ideas are eventually assessed rigorously. However, divergent thinking should not be equated with completely random thought, nor should convergent thinking be reduced to a single formula. Both activities involve judgments, knowledge, constraints, and context, and creative work can alternate between generating, testing, revising, and combining ideas repeatedly.

Study instruction:
- Solve one practical problem by recording at least five distinct ideas before evaluation, then rank them against explicit and relevant criteria.
Key point:
- Divergent thinking expands the candidate solution set.
- Convergent thinking evaluates alternatives against the problem’s requirements.
Source links:
- Divergent Thinking — Cambridge Handbook of Creativity
31. Q: Why does Fletcher question ideation as a complete explanation of innovation?
A: Show answer
Fletcher argues that generating many ideas and selecting those that resemble past successes may not fully explain how people create genuinely new approaches. His concern is that a two-step procedure can remain dependent on existing representations, categories, and criteria. If the problem is framed incorrectly, generating more possibilities within the same framework may not reveal the need for a different framework. This is an important distinction between improving candidate solutions and reconsidering what the problem actually is. However, the chapter’s criticism does not establish that ideation is useless. Structured brainstorming and evaluation can support creativity, especially when teams challenge their assumptions and revisit their evaluation criteria as they learn. The practical challenge is to combine systematic idea generation with opportunities to question the problem definition and incorporate evidence that does not fit the initial model.

Study instruction:
- Apply an ideation exercise to a problem twice: first using the original definition, then using a deliberately revised definition. Compare the ideas generated.
Key point:
- Ideation can be limited by the way a problem is framed.
- Strong creative processes permit both alternative solutions and alternative problem definitions.
Source links:
- Divergent Thinking — Cambridge Handbook of Creativity
32. Q: How does Fletcher connect ideation with design thinking and managerial problem-solving?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes how systematic approaches to generating and evaluating ideas became influential in business and education. In practical settings, these methods help teams define a challenge, generate options, test prototypes, gather feedback, and refine a proposed solution. The benefit is a repeatable process that makes creative work more manageable and easier to communicate. The chapter questions whether such methods fully capture innovation when their criteria are based too closely on familiar patterns. That concern suggests an additional practice: periodically check whether the problem definition, success measures, or assumptions need to change. A team should not continue refining a solution simply because it performs well against a metric if the metric no longer reflects the real need. Ideation is best treated as a useful toolkit within a broader process that includes exploration, testing, learning, and reassessment.

Study instruction:
- Map a design-thinking or problem-solving exercise from problem definition to testing. Identify when the team evaluates the original assumptions and when it can change them.
Key point:
- Structured methods can make ideation and testing more repeatable.
- Innovation may require reconsidering the problem definition as evidence emerges.
Source links:
- Divergent Thinking — Cambridge Handbook of Creativity
33. Q: What is Fletcher’s main criticism of generative AI as a source of innovation?
A: Show answer
Fletcher argues that generative AI combines large-scale pattern processing with the production of many candidate outputs, yet he doubts that these capabilities are sufficient for the deepest forms of original innovation. He emphasizes the difference between generating plausible material from learned patterns and discovering a new approach to a problem through adaptive action and narrative reasoning. In his account, the missing element is not merely a shortage of data or computational speed but a distinct capability associated with moto and storythinking. This is a strong theoretical position, not an uncontested conclusion about AI. Generative systems can already contribute to ideation, content creation, programming, and other creative tasks, though their performance varies across task types and evaluation methods. A balanced assessment should separate the ability to produce novel outputs from the ability to independently identify problems, establish goals, test consequences, and sustain a creative process.

Study instruction:
- Identify one task where generative AI can produce useful candidates and another where success requires external validation, physical interaction, or substantial contextual judgment.
Key point:
- Fletcher questions whether pattern-based generation fully explains innovation.
- AI capability must be evaluated by task and evidence rather than a universal claim that it can or cannot create.
Source links:
- Generative AI Performance on Divergent Thinking Tasks
34. Q: What does empirical research say about AI and creative thinking?
A: Show answer
Research findings depend on what creativity means, which tasks are measured, how outputs are evaluated, and whether a system works alone or with people. One 2024 study in Scientific Reports reported that GPT-4 outperformed human participants on several measures of divergent thinking. Such findings challenge the broad claim that AI cannot produce creative outputs. They do not show that a model can perform every stage of innovation, independently identify worthwhile problems, or reliably carry out discoveries in the real world. Research also distinguishes the quality of AI-generated ideas from the effect of AI assistance on human creativity. The chapter’s concerns about innovation remain useful as questions about process and capability, but they should be tested against evidence rather than treated as settled. A robust evaluation defines the task, establishes comparison conditions, assesses originality and usefulness, and checks whether an output works beyond the evaluation setting.

Study instruction:
- Read the cited study’s methods and results. Identify what type of creativity it measures and list two broader capabilities that the study does not directly establish.
Key point:
- AI can perform strongly on some creative-thinking tests.
- Results on one task do not establish general superiority or universal incapability.
Source links:
- The Current State of AI Generative Language Models on Divergent Thinking Tasks
35. Q: How can human-AI collaboration support creativity without replacing independent thinking?
A: Show answer
Human-AI collaboration can be useful when a person employs AI to expand the range of candidate ideas, compare possible approaches, summarize information, or identify assumptions that need checking. Human judgment remains important for setting appropriate objectives, validating facts, understanding stakeholders, recognizing practical constraints, and accepting responsibility for decisions. Fletcher’s emphasis on storythinking suggests asking not only whether an AI-generated output looks plausible but also how it would work in a real sequence of events, what could go wrong, and how the result could be tested. Research on generative AI and creativity also indicates that assistance can improve some forms of creative performance while affecting the diversity of ideas. Therefore, teams should evaluate the effects of AI use instead of assuming that it always improves creativity. Independent brainstorming before consulting AI can help preserve original approaches for comparison.

Study instruction:
- Conduct a short creative exercise independently and then with AI assistance. Compare originality, usefulness, diversity, factual accuracy, and the amount of revision needed.
Key point:
- AI can augment idea generation and evaluation.
- Collaboration should preserve independent judgment, validation, and responsibility.

36. Q: How can storythinking improve strategic planning?
A: Show answer
Strategic planning becomes more useful when it connects an objective with the sequence of decisions and actions that could plausibly achieve it. Instead of listing targets alone, a team can describe the current situation, the intended future, the obstacles between them, the actions required, and the signals that would indicate whether the plan is working. Fletcher’s approach encourages planners to consider alternative routes and imagine how unexpected events might change the sequence. For example, a product launch plan should examine not only planned milestones but also how supplier delays, customer feedback, or changes in demand would affect subsequent decisions. A narrative plan is not a substitute for budgets, forecasts, schedules, or risk analysis. It complements those tools by making dependencies and assumptions visible so the team can revise its approach when circumstances change.

Study instruction:
- Write a strategic scenario with a goal, milestones, dependencies, risks, decision points, and alternative paths. Identify measurable signs that would trigger a revision.
Key point:
- Strategy links objectives to sequences of decisions and actions.
- Plans become more resilient when assumptions and contingencies are explicit.
Source links:
- The Causal Structure and Computational Value of Narratives
37. Q: How can storythinking improve leadership and communication?
A: Show answer
Leaders often need to explain how a decision relates to an organization’s objectives, why an existing approach must change, and what employees should do next. A clear narrative can connect context, intended outcomes, action, and consequences in a way that helps people understand the purpose of a decision. Storythinking can also help leaders consider how the same event looks from different perspectives and why employees may respond differently to a change. However, narrative can be misused to simplify complex facts, manipulate emotion, or hide inconvenient evidence. Effective communication therefore combines a coherent explanation with transparent assumptions, reliable data, and opportunities for questions. Leaders should distinguish what is known, what is predicted, and what remains uncertain. The purpose is not merely to make a message compelling but to help people understand the situation and act appropriately.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a short explanation of a proposed change that includes the problem, evidence, intended outcome, practical steps, uncertainties, and how progress will be assessed.
Key point:
- Narrative can connect organizational purpose with concrete actions.
- Credible communication requires evidence, transparency, and acknowledgment of uncertainty.
Source links:
- Stories in Action
38. Q: How can storythinking help a person respond to an unexpected event?
A: Show answer
An unexpected event can invalidate assumptions on which an existing plan depends. Storythinking provides a way to reconstruct what has happened, identify which parts of the original explanation remain valid, and consider alternative paths forward. The process begins by separating observed facts from interpretations, then identifying possible causes, affected dependencies, immediate risks, and available responses. It continues by choosing a safe and proportionate action, observing the result, and updating the plan. For instance, if a critical supplier fails to deliver, a team should examine the reason, the effect on downstream milestones, and the feasibility of alternative suppliers or schedules. The purpose is not to invent an appealing story after the event but to develop a working explanation that supports responsible decisions and can be revised as new information arrives.

Study instruction:
- Use a real or hypothetical disruption to map known facts, possible causes, immediate actions, alternative scenarios, and evidence that would change your decision.
Key point:
- Unexpected events require reassessment rather than automatic repetition of the original plan.
- Explanations must remain open to correction as evidence changes.
Source links:
- The Causal Structure and Computational Value of Narratives
39. Q: What risks arise when a person relies too heavily on narrative explanations?
A: Show answer
Narratives are powerful because they connect events into coherent explanations, but coherence can be mistaken for proof. A person may select evidence that supports a preferred interpretation, overlook information that contradicts it, or assume that an outcome was inevitable simply because its causes seem clear in retrospect. Different people can also construct different narratives from the same events. These risks are particularly serious in leadership, investigations, science, and performance evaluation, where a compelling explanation can influence consequential decisions. The solution is not to abandon narratives but to discipline them. Separate evidence from inference, consider plausible alternatives, identify missing information, and establish tests that could disconfirm the preferred explanation. A useful story should remain provisional when the facts are incomplete and should change when better evidence becomes available.

Study instruction:
- Write two competing explanations for the same event and identify observations that would support one explanation while weakening the other.
Key point:
- Narrative coherence does not guarantee factual accuracy.
- Alternative explanations and disconfirming evidence improve reasoning.
Source links:
- Aristotle on Causality — Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy
40. Q: Why should logic and storythinking be treated as complementary rather than mutually exclusive?
A: Show answer
Fletcher deliberately emphasizes the differences between symbolic reasoning and narrative cognition because he believes that modern approaches can undervalue imagination and action. Nevertheless, practical reasoning benefits from both. Logic can test consistency, calculate consequences, examine quantitative evidence, and identify invalid inferences. Narrative can help organize sequences of events, explore possible futures, consider different perspectives, and imagine how actions could unfold. For example, a scientific team might use quantitative analysis to evaluate a hypothesis and narrative scenarios to identify unexpected mechanisms or test conditions. Neither method guarantees correctness: a logical model can rest on false assumptions, while a plausible story can rest on unsupported causal claims. A strong problem-solving process allows each mode to challenge the other and selects the appropriate method according to the question, evidence, constraints, and consequences.

Study instruction:
- Take one decision and analyze it twice: first with structured evidence and explicit criteria, then with causal scenarios and alternative futures. Combine the results into a final judgment.
Key point:
- Analytical reasoning and narrative imagination contribute different strengths.
- Integrating the two reduces the limitations of relying on either alone.
Source links:
- The Causal Structure and Computational Value of Narratives
41. Q: How can storythinking be developed through deliberate practice?
A: Show answer
Storythinking can be practiced by reconstructing real events, imagining alternative outcomes, and testing assumptions about how actions lead to consequences. A useful routine is to select a specific decision or incident, document what was known at the time, map the actual sequence of events, identify key choices, and consider alternative actions. The learner can then compare these interpretations with supporting evidence and use the results to improve future decisions. Fiction can provide another form of practice because it allows readers to examine complex situations without having to experience their full real-world consequences. Deliberate practice requires more than repeated exposure, however. It benefits from clear objectives, feedback, varied scenarios, and reflection on errors. Narrative exercises should be combined with relevant technical knowledge and domain-specific methods so that imaginative thinking leads to better judgments rather than speculation alone.

Study instruction:
- Complete a written review of one decision each week, recording the context, alternatives, choice, outcome, evidence, and lesson for future action.
Key point:
- Deliberate narrative practice can structure reflection and scenario planning.
- Feedback and factual review are necessary to turn reflection into learning.
Source links:
- Stories in Action
42. Q: What practical lessons does the chapter offer for scientists, engineers, and researchers?
A: Show answer
Fletcher’s model encourages researchers to combine formal analysis with curiosity about anomalies and the willingness to revise a hypothesis. Data analysis can reveal trends and correlations, while experiments, physical interaction, and creative hypothesis formation can help determine why a phenomenon occurs. The chapter’s emphasis on action also encourages the use of iterative testing: propose an explanation, design a suitable test, observe what happens, and adapt the next experiment. These practices are consistent with the broader importance of evidence-driven inquiry, although they are not unique to Fletcher’s theory of moto. Researchers must avoid treating an unusual result as proof of a revolutionary discovery before checking measurement errors, alternative explanations, and reproducibility. The useful lesson is to combine exploratory thinking with disciplined testing and to permit unexpected results to improve the research question rather than forcing them to fit the original prediction.

Study instruction:
- Review an experiment that produced an unexpected result and document the original hypothesis, the observation, possible sources of error, alternative explanations, and next test.
Key point:
- Unexpected results can lead to improved hypotheses.
- Scientific discovery requires verification, not novelty alone.

- Creativity — J. P. Guilford, American Psychologist
43. Q: What practical lessons does the chapter offer for organizations seeking innovation?
A: Show answer
Organizations can apply Fletcher’s argument by ensuring that existing metrics and procedures do not prevent employees from investigating meaningful exceptions. A structured innovation process should include problem definition, idea generation, evaluation, testing, feedback, and reassessment of assumptions. Teams should be able to report unexpected outcomes without being pressured to make every result fit the original plan. At the same time, organizations need controls to distinguish a promising new idea from an unsafe, unsupported, or impractical proposal. Managers can support this balance by making experimentation proportionate to risk, setting clear evaluation criteria, documenting lessons, and providing a route for revising objectives when evidence warrants it. The goal is neither unrestricted experimentation nor rigid adherence to rules, but a process that learns from evidence and can change direction responsibly.

Study instruction:
- Review an existing innovation process and identify how it handles failed experiments, unexpected results, dissenting opinions, and changes to the original problem definition.
Key point:
- Innovation benefits from structured experimentation and attention to exceptions.
- Controls should support responsible learning rather than suppress all departures from precedent.

44. Q: How should readers critically evaluate the chapter’s major claims about human intelligence and AI?
A: Show answer
Readers should separate the chapter’s conceptual distinctions, historical examples, biological explanations, and predictions about AI. Each type of claim requires a different evaluation method. Conceptual claims can be assessed for clarity and usefulness; historical claims require reliable records and attention to context; neuroscientific claims require appropriate empirical evidence; and predictions about AI require evaluation against actual system capabilities and well-defined benchmarks. For example, the distinction between representing an action and performing it is useful for analyzing AI capabilities, but it does not by itself demonstrate that computational systems cannot reason about causes or generate novel solutions. Similarly, examples of famous people who admired Shakespeare illustrate cultural influence but cannot establish that reading Shakespeare caused their success. A critical reader should identify the evidence supporting each claim, consider counterexamples, and distinguish a productive framework from a universal scientific law.

Study instruction:
- Select three major claims from the chapter and classify each as conceptual, historical, empirical, or predictive. Identify the evidence needed to assess its validity.
Key point:
- Different claims require different forms of evidence.
- Critical evaluation should preserve useful insights while questioning unsupported generalizations.

45. Q: What is the most important lesson to retain from Chapter 11, “Moto”?
A: Show answer
The chapter asks readers to broaden their understanding of intelligence beyond calculation and recognition of established patterns. Fletcher’s central proposal is that practical intelligence also involves imagining possible actions, organizing events into causal narratives, adapting to unfamiliar circumstances, and learning from feedback. Shakespeare serves as the chapter’s major cultural example of how narratives can explore exceptional behavior and consequential decisions. The historical discussion of optimization and generative AI extends the same argument to contemporary problem-solving: improving an existing solution is not always the same as discovering a new way forward. The most useful practical interpretation is to combine analytical rigor with imaginative exploration. Recognize patterns, but investigate exceptions; develop plans, but reconsider assumptions; generate ideas, but test them. This approach captures the chapter’s central contribution without requiring acceptance of every claim it makes about biology, consciousness, or AI.

Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter in five points covering logic, moto, storythinking, creativity, and AI. For each point, distinguish Fletcher’s argument from the evidence that supports or challenges it.
Key point:
- Effective thinking combines structured reasoning, imagination, action, and feedback.
- The chapter is most useful as a framework for reflection and critical inquiry, not as a substitute for empirical evidence.
