Table 12
Storythinking: Imagination, Creativity, and Practical Intelligence
1. Q: What is the central argument of Storythinking, and how does it change the way we understand human intelligence?
A: Show answer
Angus Fletcher argues that human intelligence involves more than calculating probabilities, evaluating known alternatives, or selecting the option that appears best from available information. People also possess a capacity to imagine possibilities that have not yet been identified, construct narratives about how situations might develop, and devise new responses when established approaches fail. He calls attention to the relationship between storytelling and practical planning: a story explores how actions, circumstances, and consequences can unfold, while a plan applies a comparable structure to real-world problems. His approach emphasizes curiosity, intuition, imagination, emotional understanding, and common sense as complementary resources for decision-making. The chapter presents this framework as a way to understand creativity and adaptability, not as proof that quantitative reasoning is unnecessary. Its central practical implication is that people should learn to generate new possibilities, examine them critically, and adjust their plans when reality changes.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between selecting an existing option and inventing a new one, then identify a real situation in which the distinction matters.
Key point:
- Practical intelligence includes generating possibilities, not merely evaluating alternatives.
- Storythinking combines imagination with action and adaptation.
2. Q: What is naturalism, and why does Fletcher consider it important for understanding intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fletcher uses naturalism to describe a method of studying individual phenomena through patient observation, curiosity, and close attention to unusual characteristics. Instead of beginning with a generalized model of what normally happens, a naturalist studies a particular case, notices what makes it distinctive, and investigates the mechanisms that might explain its behavior. The method requires more than collecting observations. It also involves developing informed hypotheses about processes that cannot be seen directly. Fletcher presents this approach as particularly useful for examining abilities such as imagination, intuition, emotion, and common sense, which may be difficult to understand through population averages alone. Naturalism does not establish that individual observations are automatically more reliable than statistical evidence. Its value lies in identifying important questions, unexpected mechanisms, and possibilities that broader measurement may overlook. Later investigation can test those ideas using other methods.
Study instruction:
- Choose an unusual behavior or unexpected outcome and outline how you would observe it, investigate its context, and develop explanations before reaching a conclusion.
Key point:
- Naturalism begins with careful observation of particular cases.
- Imaginative explanations require subsequent evaluation and testing.
3. Q: Why should researchers pay particular attention to unusual cases and unexpected behavior?
A: Show answer
Unusual cases may reveal mechanisms or possibilities that familiar examples do not expose. When an observation differs from an established expectation, it creates an opportunity to investigate why the current explanation is incomplete. Fletcher describes curiosity as an essential starting point: an anomaly attracts attention, encourages closer observation, and motivates the investigator to consider alternative explanations. For example, a product might succeed with a group that the original research predicted would reject it. Rather than dismissing those customers as statistically insignificant, a researcher could investigate their circumstances, motivations, and actual behavior. The resulting discovery might reveal a new use case or a mistaken assumption about the wider market. However, an exceptional case does not establish a universal rule by itself. The appropriate response is to investigate the anomaly, identify plausible mechanisms, and determine whether further observations support the explanation.
Study instruction:
- Record one unexpected result from a real project and develop three plausible explanations that could be tested with additional evidence.
Key point:
- Anomalies can expose weaknesses in existing explanations.
- An unusual observation is a starting point for inquiry, not conclusive proof.
4. Q: How does studying individual cases differ from studying an average or representative person?
A: Show answer
Statistical research often aggregates observations to identify patterns across a population. This can reveal meaningful tendencies, relationships, and differences between groups. However, an aggregate can conceal the distinctive processes, circumstances, and motivations that produce a particular outcome. An average customer, employee, or learner does not necessarily explain why an unusual individual behaves differently. Fletcher favors close examination of exceptional cases when the research objective is to understand how a distinctive capacity develops or operates. In practice, the methods are complementary. Large datasets can identify recurring patterns and test whether an observed relationship extends beyond a small sample, while individual case studies can reveal context, generate new hypotheses, and clarify mechanisms. A sound investigation chooses its method according to the question rather than assuming that either a population average or a single remarkable case provides the complete explanation.
Study instruction:
- Compare a team-wide performance report with a detailed investigation of one unusually successful or unsuccessful project. Identify what each method can and cannot explain.
Key point:
- Averages reveal population-level patterns but can conceal individual mechanisms.
- Case studies add depth and context without replacing broader validation.
5. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of statistical approaches to studying human intelligence?
A: Show answer
Statistical methods are valuable for examining large datasets, estimating relationships, evaluating uncertainty, and determining whether observed patterns are likely to reflect broader tendencies. They can help researchers avoid relying entirely on anecdotes or personal impressions. Their limitations arise when the available measurements fail to capture an important aspect of the problem, when the underlying environment changes, or when a general pattern is mistaken for an explanation of an individual case. Fletcher challenges the assumption that more calculation necessarily produces a complete understanding of intelligence. The practical lesson is not to abandon statistics but to recognize the questions they can answer. Quantitative evidence can establish how often something happens under specified conditions, while close observation and causal investigation may help explain how a distinctive result occurs. Strong analysis combines these approaches where appropriate and makes the limitations of each explicit.
Study instruction:
- For a decision you have made using a dashboard, identify the quantitative evidence available, the contextual information missing, and the additional observation that would improve the decision.
Key point:
- Statistics are powerful for measuring patterns and evaluating evidence.
- Numerical precision does not automatically provide a complete explanation of behavior.
6. Q: How can intuitive observation and mathematical analysis complement one another?
A: Show answer
Fletcher presents naturalistic investigation and mathematical analysis as complementary stages of discovery. Careful observation can reveal a surprising result, while imagination can generate hypotheses about the mechanism behind it. Mathematical or experimental methods can then test those hypotheses, estimate their generality, and identify the conditions under which they hold. The process can also operate in reverse: a statistical anomaly may direct attention toward an unusual case that deserves closer study. For example, an organization might detect an unexpected increase in project delays through its performance dashboard. Investigating individual projects could reveal that several experienced a previously unrecognized logistical obstacle. The organization could then test whether the same obstacle explains delays elsewhere. The important distinction is between generating an explanation and establishing that it is correct. Intuition can guide discovery, but evidence must determine how much confidence the explanation deserves.
Study instruction:
- Design a two-stage investigation for a surprising workplace result: first investigate individual cases, then specify how you would test the resulting hypothesis across a larger dataset.
Key point:
- Observation and imagination help generate useful hypotheses.
- Testing determines whether the proposed explanation is sufficiently supported.
7. Q: What does Michael Faraday's example illustrate about scientific discovery?
A: Show answer
Fletcher uses Faraday to illustrate the possibility that careful, imaginative engagement with a problem can produce discoveries before their mathematical formulation is available. Faraday's experimental investigations of electromagnetic phenomena helped establish foundations later expressed through mathematical theories associated with James Clerk Maxwell. The example illustrates a distinction between discovering a relationship and formally describing it. A researcher may notice patterns, develop an intuitive understanding, and investigate physical mechanisms before a complete mathematical account exists. This does not mean mathematics is unnecessary for scientific validity. Quantitative formulation can clarify relationships, predict results, and make findings easier to test and communicate. The broader lesson is that discovery can originate through different methods, and an unfamiliar or initially nonmathematical approach should be evaluated on the quality of its observations, explanations, and eventual tests rather than dismissed solely because it follows a different path.
Study instruction:
- Identify an innovation in your field that originated from observation or experimentation, then distinguish the discovery process from its later formal validation.
Key point:
- Important discoveries can precede their formal mathematical explanation.
- Intuitive discovery and rigorous validation serve different but complementary purposes.
8. Q: Why does Fletcher discuss William James when explaining a naturalistic approach to psychology?
A: Show answer
Fletcher presents William James as an example of an influential thinker who investigated psychological questions through close attention to individual experience rather than relying exclusively on statistical generalization. James's work explored subjects including consciousness, habit, emotion, belief, and human agency. In Fletcher's account, this approach illustrates how detailed attention to individual psychological phenomena can generate questions and explanations that later researchers may investigate through additional methods. The example is not evidence that statistics lack value or that every claim derived from introspection is correct. It instead demonstrates that understanding human experience may require multiple forms of inquiry. Researchers need methods capable of examining both broad patterns and the particular experiences that give those patterns meaning. This is especially relevant when the objective is to understand how people interpret circumstances, construct possibilities, or adapt to unfamiliar situations.
Study instruction:
- Choose a psychological or behavioral question and explain what a detailed individual case could reveal that a survey average might leave unexplored.
Key point:
- Individual experience can generate important psychological questions.
- Case-based interpretation still requires suitable evidence and critical evaluation.
9. Q: What did Fletcher learn from studying individual synapses, and how did this influence his approach to intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes his early neuroscience work as an attempt to understand how individual synapses functioned and how changing their mechanisms affected their behavior. Studying distinctive units closely encouraged him to focus on the processes that generated observable actions rather than assuming that a large collection of averaged measurements would reveal everything important. He subsequently extended this perspective to whole brains, asking how an individual's particular capacities might be understood through careful investigation. The crucial conceptual step was to consider actions as sequences connected through time and to investigate how narratives represent such sequences. The chapter develops this idea into the proposition that story can provide a model for examining imaginative and practical thought. This is the author's theoretical framework, not a demonstration that every neural process operates like a conventional story. Its value lies in suggesting a productive way to examine the connections between perception, action, interpretation, and planning.
Study instruction:
- Take a complex behavior and break it into a sequence of observable actions, possible internal processes, and contextual factors that might explain the sequence.
Key point:
- Understanding a complex system can begin with careful examination of its components and mechanisms.
- Narrative is presented as a framework for investigating connected actions and possibilities.
10. Q: What does it mean to say that the brain thinks in narrative?
A: Show answer
Fletcher proposes that narrative offers a useful framework for understanding certain forms of human thought. In a narrative, events are connected through actions, circumstances, motivations, and consequences. Similarly, practical thinking often involves imagining what might happen if someone takes a particular action, how other people might respond, and what consequences could follow. This connection allows researchers and practitioners to examine imagination as a process of constructing possible sequences rather than merely selecting among fixed answers. The claim should be interpreted carefully: the brain performs many different functions, and the chapter's narrative framework does not establish that all cognition is literally organized as a story. Its practical significance is that people can use narrative structures to explore complex situations, consider alternative futures, recognize causal relationships, and generate plans that adapt when circumstances change.
Study instruction:
- Describe an upcoming challenge as a sequence of actions, reactions, obstacles, and possible outcomes. Create at least two alternative versions of the sequence.
Key point:
- Narrative can model how actions and consequences connect over time.
- Storythinking is a framework for practical reasoning, not a complete account of every brain function.
11. Q: How can the structure of a story help someone reason about a practical problem?
A: Show answer
A story provides a structure for examining a situation as it develops. It can identify the people involved, the circumstances they face, the actions they take, the obstacles they encounter, and the consequences that follow. This makes narrative useful for examining problems that cannot be solved through a static description alone. For example, a project manager planning a delayed program can imagine how a change in delivery arrangements might affect staff, suppliers, beneficiaries, deadlines, and costs. The exercise reveals dependencies and possible reactions that a simple list of options may not capture. A story does not guarantee an accurate forecast, because imagined sequences rely on assumptions about how the world will behave. It becomes a practical reasoning tool when those assumptions are examined, alternative sequences are developed, and the resulting plan is checked against operational evidence and constraints.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite one work problem as a short scenario containing the current situation, an intended action, two obstacles, and several possible consequences.
Key point:
- Narrative makes relationships between actions and consequences easier to explore.
- Imagined outcomes must be checked against real-world constraints.
12. Q: Why did Fletcher choose Shakespeare as a subject for investigating human imagination?
A: Show answer
Fletcher was interested in Shakespeare because the playwright produced unusually influential stories and appeared to develop distinctive ways of constructing them. Rather than studying only the patterns common to many successful plays, Fletcher wanted to investigate an exceptional creator whose work might reveal how innovative storytelling develops. Shakespeare also provided a bridge between narrative and practical planning. His career involved not only writing but also adapting to commercial circumstances and changing theatrical conditions. Fletcher therefore approached Shakespeare as a possible source of insight into how people create options under uncertainty. Studying a famous individual, however, cannot by itself establish a universal theory of intelligence. The productive question is what mechanisms can plausibly be identified, how the examples support them, and whether the resulting ideas can be applied or independently tested in other settings.
Study instruction:
- Select an innovative person in your field and identify specific decisions, experiments, and changes in approach that might explain the person's achievements better than the person's reputation alone.
Key point:
- Exceptional creators can provide useful cases for investigating innovation.
- Their success should be examined through mechanisms and evidence rather than attributed automatically to innate talent.
13. Q: What can be gained by studying an exceptional creator over an extended period?
A: Show answer
Extended immersion can reveal patterns that are difficult to identify from a brief interview, a finished product, or a statistical summary. By repeatedly examining a creator's work, decisions, experiments, revisions, and responses to difficulties, a researcher can investigate how ideas develop and how the creator changes direction. Fletcher describes his own approach to literature as a form of sustained inquiry into Shakespeare's work and methods. In practical research, immersion should include more than admiring successful outcomes. Researchers need to study unsuccessful attempts, alternative explanations, working conditions, collaboration, and the influence of historical or institutional circumstances. They should also distinguish documented facts from interpretations. An exceptional case is particularly useful when it generates a new hypothesis, but the hypothesis must be examined independently before being treated as a broadly applicable principle.
Study instruction:
- Build a case-study outline for an innovator that includes early work, failures, turning points, collaborators, constraints, and evidence supporting your conclusions.
Key point:
- Long-term observation reveals processes and changes that snapshots may miss.
- A detailed case study generates insights that may require broader testing.
14. Q: How did studying Shakespeare lead Fletcher toward a theory of storytelling as a method of innovation?
A: Show answer
Fletcher came to view Shakespeare as more than a skilled writer who produced compelling language. He interpreted the playwright's career and creative development as evidence of a method for repeatedly changing narrative approaches. In this account, innovation involves recognizing when established patterns are insufficient and constructing alternative ways for events and characters to develop. Fletcher later found similarities between this interpretation and the methods used by certain innovative screenwriters. The broader lesson is that creative achievement may depend on a repeatable process of experimentation rather than the possession of a mysterious, unteachable gift. This interpretation should not be mistaken for a settled historical proof of Shakespeare's exact creative process. Its practical contribution is to encourage students of innovation to examine how creators revise assumptions, explore new structures, and respond to circumstances that render familiar solutions less effective.
Study instruction:
- Compare two works by a creator and identify a meaningful change in structure, assumptions, or problem-solving approach rather than merely differences in style.
Key point:
- Innovation can involve changing the method used to generate solutions.
- Creative processes should be investigated rather than assumed to be entirely innate.
15. Q: What does Fletcher's experience with Pixar illustrate about originality and storytelling?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes Pixar as an example of a creative organization willing to explore story structures that did not simply follow familiar commercial formulas. In his account, the studio's approach supported the development of distinctive films by allowing writers to experiment with alternative ways of constructing narratives. The example illustrates a broader principle: successful innovation may require an organization to tolerate uncertainty rather than require every project to reproduce a previously validated format. Established formulas can be useful because they make expectations and production requirements more predictable. However, they can also constrain experimentation if treated as mandatory rules. An innovative organization needs both a foundation of craft and the freedom to explore departures from convention. Originality still needs evaluation, including whether the resulting work communicates effectively and serves its intended audience.
Study instruction:
- Examine a successful product or creative project and identify which conventions it retained, which it changed, and how those changes contributed to its distinctive value.
Key point:
- Effective innovation can arise from experimenting beyond established formulas.
- Departing from convention is useful only when the resulting solution works for its purpose.
16. Q: What is the difference between using a conventional story formula and inventing a new story structure?
A: Show answer
A conventional formula provides familiar expectations about how a narrative is organized. It can make writing more efficient, help audiences understand a work, and reduce some creative risks. An original structure begins by questioning whether those familiar expectations are appropriate for the particular idea being developed. It may reorganize events, change the focus of the conflict, or explore a different relationship between characters and circumstances. Fletcher favors examining innovative works for the ways they change narrative possibilities rather than assuming that all successful stories follow one universal model. In practical terms, a formula is a useful starting point when it fits the problem, but it becomes restrictive when creators apply it without considering alternatives. The aim is not novelty at any cost. It is to discover a structure that expresses the central idea effectively and gives the audience a meaningful experience.
Study instruction:
- Take a familiar narrative or presentation format and design an alternative structure that better communicates a specific idea. Compare the benefits and risks of both approaches.
Key point:
- Established structures provide useful guidance but need not dictate every solution.
- Originality should improve relevance, effectiveness, or insight rather than serve as an end in itself.
17. Q: Why does Fletcher describe strategy, operations, and marketing as three related organizational stories?
A: Show answer
Fletcher proposes that strategy describes what an organization intends to accomplish, operations describe what it actually does, and marketing describes what it communicates to others about its activities. These three perspectives are connected because an organization's public claims should be consistent with its objectives and real capabilities. For example, a company may announce that it prioritizes customer service, but its operating processes may reward employees exclusively for speed and sales volume. Marketing can then create an impression that the actual service experience fails to support. Examining these dimensions together helps leaders discover inconsistencies that remain hidden when strategy, operations, and communication are reviewed separately. The framework is useful as a diagnostic tool, although the three areas also require distinct performance measures and responsibilities. Organizational alignment means checking that stated intentions, daily decisions, and external claims reinforce rather than contradict one another.
Study instruction:
- Select an organization and write three short descriptions: what it promises to accomplish, what its processes actually reward, and what it tells stakeholders. Identify inconsistencies.
Key point:
- Strategy, operations, and marketing describe different but interconnected aspects of organizational behavior.
- Credible communication depends on alignment between promises and actual performance.
18. Q: What happens when an organization's strategy, operations, and marketing tell conflicting stories?
A: Show answer
Conflicting organizational stories can undermine credibility, misdirect resources, and create confusion among employees and stakeholders. If leaders announce a long-term objective but reward behaviors that undermine it, employees may follow the incentives rather than the stated strategy. If marketing promises a capability that operations cannot deliver, customers may experience repeated disappointment. These problems are not merely communication failures; they can reflect structural weaknesses in priorities, management systems, staffing, or resource allocation. A useful response is to trace each public claim back to the processes and decisions that should make it true. Leaders should identify the gap, determine its causes, assign responsibility, and establish evidence that the gap is closing. Sometimes the organization should change its operations; in other cases, it must revise an unrealistic promise or strategic objective. Honest alignment requires both operational improvement and accurate communication.
Study instruction:
- Identify one organizational promise and map the processes, resources, incentives, and performance measures needed to support it.
Key point:
- Inconsistent intentions, actions, and claims damage organizational credibility.
- Alignment requires changes to the underlying process, not just revised messaging.
19. Q: Which three groups of executives does Fletcher describe, and what assumptions do they share?
A: Show answer
Fletcher characterizes three broad groups: conventional rationalists who emphasize quantitative management and economic models, rational humanists who emphasize empathy and user-centered design, and technology-oriented futurists who expect increasingly capable computers to outperform human decision-makers. Despite their differences, he argues that they share an underlying emphasis on rational choice: intelligence is primarily the ability to evaluate information and select an appropriate option. Fletcher believes this common assumption undervalues the capacity to invent possibilities that are not already represented in available information. The categories are the author's interpretive framework rather than a comprehensive classification of every executive or organization. Their practical value is to prompt leaders to examine their own assumptions. An organization may invest heavily in analytics, design research, or artificial intelligence while still neglecting the creative work required to imagine new strategic directions.
Study instruction:
- Examine a recent organizational decision and identify how quantitative analysis, user understanding, automation, and the generation of new options each influenced the outcome.
Key point:
- Different management philosophies can share an emphasis on choosing among existing options.
- Strategic innovation also requires examining how new options are generated.
20. Q: Why does Fletcher challenge the idea that rational decision-making is a complete model of intelligence?
A: Show answer
Rational decision-making is valuable when the decision-maker can identify the available alternatives, establish relevant criteria, estimate outcomes, and compare the choices. Fletcher argues that this approach is incomplete when the most useful alternative has not yet been conceived or when the circumstances themselves are changing. In those situations, evaluating the current options may produce a well-reasoned decision without revealing the opportunity that matters most. A team may compare existing suppliers efficiently yet fail to consider redesigning its delivery process or removing the underlying need for a particular service. The limitation is not that rational analysis is irrational or useless. It is that evaluating options and inventing options are different activities. Effective judgment can combine structured analysis with exploration, experimentation, and the imagination needed to redefine the problem.
Study instruction:
- Choose a decision with several established options and brainstorm a solution that changes the problem itself. Evaluate both the original options and the new possibility.
Key point:
- Rational comparison evaluates available choices but may not generate missing alternatives.
- Reframing a problem can create opportunities that comparison alone overlooks.
21. Q: What does Fletcher mean when he says that data is backward-looking?
A: Show answer
Fletcher's argument is that data records or summarizes observations from circumstances that have already occurred. Historical information can be highly valuable for understanding patterns, evaluating performance, and forecasting outcomes when relevant conditions remain sufficiently stable. However, new technologies, changing behavior, unusual events, and strategic interventions can alter those conditions. A model based entirely on previous experience may therefore fail when the system changes. This does not mean that all data becomes useless whenever the future differs from the past. Historical evidence remains a critical input, and models can be updated to incorporate new observations. The practical challenge is to recognize when a decision requires more than extrapolation. Leaders should consider what may change, how current assumptions might fail, and what new possibilities should be tested before making a commitment.
Study instruction:
- Find a forecast or operational assumption based on historical trends. List the conditions that must remain true for it to remain reliable and the warning signs that would justify revising it.
Key point:
- Historical data supports decisions but cannot guarantee future conditions.
- Changing circumstances require reassessment of assumptions and models.
22. Q: How does imagination help people deal with situations that have no established solution?
A: Show answer
Imagination allows people to explore possible actions and consequences before the outcome is known. When existing procedures fail, a person can consider how the situation might be changed, what resources could be combined differently, and how other people might respond to an unfamiliar approach. Fletcher treats this capacity as a central component of practical intelligence because it helps people create alternatives rather than remain confined to previous examples. Imagination is not the same as wishful thinking. A useful imagined possibility must be translated into a coherent plan and examined for feasibility, unintended effects, and evidence of success. People can then conduct small experiments or seek further information before committing significant resources. In this way, imagination expands the range of possibilities while practical evaluation keeps the exploration grounded in reality.
Study instruction:
- Identify an unresolved problem and create three possible approaches: one based on current procedures, one that modifies those procedures, and one that reframes the problem entirely.
Key point:
- Imagination generates candidate responses to unfamiliar situations.
- Feasibility checks and experimentation help distinguish useful ideas from unrealistic ones.
23. Q: What is the difference between choosing a plan and inventing a plan?
A: Show answer
Choosing a plan involves evaluating existing alternatives against a goal or a set of criteria. Inventing a plan involves constructing a new way to reach the goal, redefining the problem, or discovering an objective that was not previously considered. Fletcher draws a connection between this creative act and storytelling: a narrative can imagine a sequence of actions that leads from an initial condition to a different outcome, while a practical plan uses a comparable structure to guide real activity. Organizations often need both capabilities. A team may use comparison methods to select the best available delivery method, but if every option is too expensive or slow, the team may need to invent an alternative operating model. Once that new model exists, quantitative analysis can help evaluate it. Creation and selection are distinct stages that work best when neither is overlooked.
Study instruction:
- Describe a recent decision and separate the work involved in generating alternatives from the work involved in evaluating them.
Key point:
- Selection identifies a preferred option from available alternatives.
- Invention expands the alternatives that can be evaluated.
24. Q: How can storytelling become a tool for manipulation rather than a tool for intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fletcher questions the idea that narratives should primarily be used to steer audiences toward decisions already selected by the communicator. Stories can help people understand complicated circumstances, but they can also create misleading impressions, conceal inconvenient information, or influence behavior without encouraging independent judgment. In organizational settings, a persuasive presentation might describe a project as successful while omitting its costs, implementation failures, or effects on other stakeholders. Such communication can create agreement without producing sound decisions. A more responsible use of narrative makes its assumptions visible, acknowledges material uncertainty, and allows evidence to challenge the proposed interpretation. Storytelling should help people explore possibilities and consequences, not merely bypass their judgment. Ethical communication requires consistency between the narrative and the underlying facts, as well as a willingness to revise the story when new evidence appears.
Study instruction:
- Review a persuasive business presentation or public message. Identify omitted perspectives, unsupported causal claims, and information a reader would need to assess the argument independently.
Key point:
- Narratives can clarify complex issues or manipulate perceptions.
- Responsible storytelling preserves transparency, evidence, and independent judgment.
25. Q: Why does Fletcher connect imagination with leadership and organizational strategy?
A: Show answer
Leadership often involves deciding what an organization should become when its future cannot be determined simply by extending existing trends. Leaders need to understand current capabilities and constraints, but they must also imagine opportunities, risks, and operating models that do not yet exist. Fletcher connects this responsibility with the creation of stories and plans, emphasizing the ability to envision a different sequence of events and organize action around it. A useful strategic vision is not simply an inspiring description of a desirable future. It explains how the organization might move toward that future, which assumptions must hold, what obstacles may arise, and what evidence will indicate progress. Leaders must also invite challenge so that the vision does not become detached from reality. Imagination generates direction, while operational discipline and feedback help convert that direction into achievable change.
Study instruction:
- Draft a future-state scenario for an organization, identify the assumptions supporting it, and outline the first three actions needed to test whether the scenario is achievable.
Key point:
- Leadership requires creating plausible directions for change, not just evaluating current performance.
- A useful vision connects an imagined future to actions, assumptions, and feedback.
26. Q: Why might a person who appears naturally gifted actually be using a learnable method?
A: Show answer
Fletcher explains that other people interpreted his consulting insights as evidence of an innate talent for business. He rejected this explanation because he understood his own success as the result of methods developed through years of studying narrative. This contrast illustrates the difference between observing impressive performance and understanding how it was produced. An individual may appear to make unusually good decisions because the person has learned to notice relevant details, imagine unfamiliar possibilities, or recognize patterns that others have not yet considered. Observers who see only the final result may mistake practiced methods for effortless intuition. This does not mean that natural abilities and individual differences never matter. It means that observers should investigate the contribution of experience, tools, feedback, and deliberate practice before concluding that the result depends on an unteachable gift. Making methods explicit helps others develop the capability rather than merely admire the performer.
Study instruction:
- Choose someone known for solving difficult problems. Interview or observe the person to identify repeatable actions, questions, habits, and tools that contribute to the performance.
Key point:
- Impressive results do not establish that a capability is innate or unteachable.
- Identifying repeatable methods creates opportunities for training and improvement.
27. Q: What does Fletcher's discussion of IQ tests and standardized examinations reveal about the limits of measured ability?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes himself as highly effective at standardized tests even when the results did not accurately represent the breadth of his knowledge or practical competence. He uses this experience to challenge the assumption that excellence on a narrow assessment necessarily predicts success in complex real-world situations. Tests can measure important abilities within defined conditions, but performance may also depend on preparation, familiarity with the format, and specific test-taking strategies. Meanwhile, real-world work may require judgment under uncertainty, adaptation to changing circumstances, communication, experimentation, and recognition of when a question itself is poorly framed. The appropriate conclusion is not that standardized testing has no value. Rather, a measure should be interpreted according to what it actually assesses. A fuller evaluation of competence may require observed performance, relevant experience, practical exercises, and evidence of learning across varied situations.
Study instruction:
- Take a skill assessed in a classroom or standardized test and specify two additional forms of evidence that would show whether the skill transfers to practical performance.
Key point:
- Test performance represents a particular form of measured ability.
- Real-world competence may require additional capabilities that the test does not capture.
28. Q: Why is selecting the correct answer not the same as solving a real-world problem?
A: Show answer
Selecting the correct answer assumes that the problem and its candidate responses have already been specified. Solving an unfamiliar real-world problem may require identifying the actual issue, collecting relevant observations, challenging the initial framing, and developing an alternative that was not included in the original choices. Fletcher emphasizes this distinction through his account of standardized tests and his later work on planning. A person can be excellent at evaluating predefined answers yet struggle when the situation is ambiguous and no established response is suitable. Conversely, someone who initially struggles to compare abstract alternatives may be effective at recognizing concrete opportunities and inventing practical solutions. These capabilities overlap but are not identical. Education and professional training should therefore develop both accurate analysis and the ability to define problems, generate alternatives, and test proposed solutions under changing conditions.
Study instruction:
- Turn a multiple-choice problem from your field into an open-ended exercise that requires defining the issue and proposing at least two previously unspecified solutions.
Key point:
- Answer selection operates within an existing problem structure.
- Problem-solving may require inventing the structure and available options first.
29. Q: How are stories, plots, and plans connected in Fletcher's framework?
A: Show answer
Fletcher connects these concepts through their shared concern with how events can develop over time. A plot arranges actions and consequences into a meaningful sequence. A plan similarly organizes actions toward a desired result, taking account of circumstances, obstacles, and other actors. A story can therefore provide a way to examine the logic of a prospective plan: what must happen first, what might interrupt progress, how other people might respond, and what alternatives remain available. This connection helps explain why narrative analysis can be useful outside literature. Managers, engineers, clinicians, and entrepreneurs routinely imagine sequences of actions before carrying them out. The analogy has limits, because real situations involve uncertainty, competing objectives, and consequences that cannot always be anticipated. Nevertheless, treating a plan as a developing sequence makes assumptions and dependencies easier to examine and revise.
Study instruction:
- Describe a real project as a plot with an initial situation, an objective, obstacles, decision points, and alternative routes to completion.
Key point:
- Both plots and plans organize actions and potential consequences over time.
- Treating plans as developing sequences improves the examination of dependencies and alternatives.
30. Q: How can learning from fictional characters improve the generation of practical strategies?
A: Show answer
Fletcher suggests that stories can stimulate imagination by presenting characters who confront obstacles, make consequential choices, and respond to changing circumstances. Readers can mentally explore what those characters might do in an unfamiliar situation and then use that perspective to consider their own decisions. His discussion of Shakespeare includes the possibility that a reader or writer learns to create plans by studying a character who is skilled at plotting. The mechanism is not that fictional characters provide universally correct instructions. Instead, narrative can provide a safe setting for exploring approaches, recognizing motives, and considering the consequences of different actions. Practical application requires translating the insight into the actual circumstances of the decision-maker. A strategy that works in a fictional conflict may be inappropriate in a workplace or community setting because the goals, ethical obligations, and constraints differ.
Study instruction:
- Choose a fictional character who handles uncertainty effectively. Identify one transferable problem-solving technique and one reason the technique might not work in your own context.
Key point:
- Fiction can provide mental models for exploring alternative responses.
- Any strategy borrowed from a story must be adapted to real constraints and ethical responsibilities.
31. Q: What does the movement from imitation to originality teach us about creative development?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes Shakespeare's early work as relying substantially on existing stories and styles before the playwright developed a more distinctive creative approach. In Fletcher's interpretation, Shakespeare's development involved more than producing novel language. It involved changing how stories were constructed and how narrative possibilities were explored. This suggests that studying and adapting existing work can form part of learning, but continued development requires more than copying successful precedents. A creator must understand what a method accomplishes, recognize where it becomes limiting, and experiment with alternatives. Originality does not require creating without any influence; it requires transforming what has been learned into a solution appropriate to a new situation. The same process can occur in professional work when teams study established methods and then modify them in response to constraints, opportunities, or needs that previous examples did not address.
Study instruction:
- Examine a standard procedure in your field and identify which elements should remain, which could be adapted, and which assumptions deserve testing through a new approach.
Key point:
- Imitation can provide a starting point for acquiring skill.
- Originality develops through informed adaptation and the creation of effective alternatives.
32. Q: Why can a major crisis create opportunities for innovation?
A: Show answer
A crisis can disrupt the conditions that allowed established practices to work. When demand disappears, resources become unavailable, or an existing delivery model stops functioning, organizations may be forced to reconsider assumptions that previously seemed unquestionable. Fletcher uses Shakespeare's career during a period of plague-related disruption to illustrate how severe external changes can coincide with creative and strategic transformation. The practical lesson is not that crisis automatically produces innovation or that disruption should be welcomed without qualification. Crises may destroy resources, harm people, and narrow the range of available choices. They can nevertheless reveal the inadequacy of an old model and make experimentation necessary. An effective response begins by protecting people and essential operations, assessing new constraints, identifying viable possibilities, and testing revised plans. Adaptation depends on how the organization responds, not simply on the existence of adversity.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a past disruption and distinguish its immediate harms, the assumptions it invalidated, and the adaptations that helped a team recover or improve.
Key point:
- Disruption can expose the limits of established approaches.
- Innovation requires deliberate adaptation; crisis alone does not guarantee improvement.
33. Q: How can constraints encourage creative thinking without making a solution unrealistic?
A: Show answer
Constraints force decision-makers to confront what cannot be changed easily, including budgets, deadlines, available skills, legal requirements, and operational capacity. They can encourage creative alternatives because the simplest familiar solution may no longer be feasible. Storythinking can help a team explore different sequences of actions and imagine ways to meet an objective within those limits. However, a constraint should not be treated as an invitation to ignore essential safety, ethical, or legal obligations. Nor should creativity be confused with assuming that insufficient resources can always be overcome. A practical process distinguishes fixed requirements from flexible assumptions, explores alternative arrangements, evaluates the risks, and tests whether the proposed approach can work at the required scale. When the constraints make the objective impossible, the responsible response may be to revise the objective or seek additional resources.
Study instruction:
- Choose a project with a resource limitation. Separate non-negotiable requirements from flexible arrangements and develop three feasible alternatives.
Key point:
- Constraints can prompt useful experimentation and reframing.
- Creative solutions must still satisfy essential requirements and realistic resource limits.
34. Q: What does Fletcher's collaboration with U.S. Army Special Operations illustrate about practical intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fletcher describes Special Operations personnel as people who work in unfamiliar environments over extended periods, observe local conditions, and search for solutions to problems that cannot be resolved through routine procedures alone. He connects their approach with naturalism because both depend on immersion, attention to particular circumstances, and the generation of new possibilities. The chapter also challenges the stereotype that exceptional performance must originate from people with extraordinary innate abilities. Fletcher reports that the operators viewed their achievements as products of methods, training, tools, and teamwork rather than evidence that they were born fundamentally different. The broader lesson is that complex work requires both individual judgment and a system that supports people in gathering information, adapting plans, and learning from experience. Because operational contexts differ substantially, the exact methods used in one environment must not be transferred blindly into another.
Study instruction:
- Examine a professional role that operates under uncertainty and identify how field observation, local context, contingency planning, and team coordination contribute to effective decisions.
Key point:
- Practical intelligence often depends on adaptation to specific conditions.
- Training, tools, and team processes can support performance beyond individual intuition.
35. Q: Why does transferring knowledge to a team matter more than relying on a single expert?
A: Show answer
Fletcher recounts that the Special Operations team wanted his methods communicated in a form that others could learn and apply. This reflects a fundamental organizational risk: knowledge that exists only in one person's head can become unavailable when that person leaves, becomes incapacitated, or is no longer involved. A resilient organization makes important reasoning processes teachable, documents relevant decisions, allows others to practice the methods, and builds sufficient redundancy for work to continue. Transfer requires more than writing down conclusions. It includes explaining how an expert recognizes a problem, tests assumptions, evaluates alternatives, and responds when an approach fails. Teams should also learn when a method is inappropriate and when an issue requires specialist judgment. The goal is not to make expertise unnecessary, but to distribute useful capabilities and prevent critical decisions from depending entirely on one individual.
Study instruction:
- Select a process currently dependent on one experienced person and develop a training outline that captures the decision steps, common exceptions, and escalation criteria.
Key point:
- Undocumented expertise creates continuity and operational risks.
- Knowledge transfer should cover reasoning, application, limitations, and exception handling.
36. Q: What can the episode involving Fletcher's broken glasses teach us about unexpected problems?
A: Show answer
Fletcher recounts that damage to his glasses before military training created a problem he was too embarrassed and anxious to report. Instead of requesting assistance, he attempted to conceal the situation and continue as though nothing had changed. This decision made later tasks much more difficult and contributed to additional consequences. The episode illustrates how a small initial problem can become substantially more serious when someone hides it, avoids asking for help, or continues operating under a false assumption. In workplaces, comparable behavior may occur when employees conceal missing documentation, an error in a calculation, a system failure, or a deadline that can no longer be met. The solution is not to eliminate personal accountability, but to create a response process that prioritizes early disclosure, accurate assessment, proportionate correction, and learning. Concealment generally reduces the time and information available for a safe recovery.
Study instruction:
- Consider a situation in which a minor problem became more serious. Identify the earliest point at which disclosure or assistance could have reduced the consequences.
Key point:
- Concealing an unexpected problem can compound its effects.
- Prompt disclosure gives the team more opportunities to correct the situation safely.
37. Q: How can shame and fear prevent people from making effective decisions?
A: Show answer
Shame and fear can narrow a person's willingness to examine a problem openly. Someone who believes that admitting difficulty will damage their reputation may conceal information, avoid asking for help, or continue with an approach that is no longer viable. Fletcher's personal account illustrates how an attempt to appear competent can conflict with the practical need to acknowledge a limitation. In organizations, this pattern can undermine reporting, safety, financial controls, and team learning. Managers can reduce the risk by responding constructively to early disclosures, distinguishing honest mistakes from deliberate misconduct, and establishing clear routes for escalation. This does not mean that every error should be free of consequences. It means that people should not face incentives so punitive or unpredictable that they are encouraged to hide material problems. Effective judgment requires access to reality, including uncomfortable facts about one's own abilities or decisions.
Study instruction:
- Review how your team handles mistakes and identify one change that would make early reporting safer while preserving responsibility for corrective action.
Key point:
- Fear of appearing incompetent can encourage concealment and poor decisions.
- A sound reporting culture supports early disclosure, correction, and accountability.
38. Q: What should someone do when an unexpected event invalidates the current plan?
A: Show answer
The first step is to acknowledge the change rather than continue acting as if the original assumptions remain true. The decision-maker should assess immediate risks, identify which objectives and requirements remain valid, and determine what information is needed before proceeding. Next, the person or team should generate alternatives, considering how different actions could affect other people, resources, and future outcomes. When time permits, a small test can help distinguish a workable response from an attractive but unrealistic idea. The plan should then be revised, communicated to relevant participants, and monitored for unintended consequences. Fletcher's account of his military experience illustrates the risks of pretending that a serious obstacle does not exist. The general principle is to expose the problem early, maintain situational awareness, and adapt deliberately rather than allowing fear or commitment to the original plan to dictate the response.
Study instruction:
- Build a contingency plan for a realistic disruption, including an initial safety assessment, three response options, decision criteria, and a point at which the plan must be reviewed.
Key point:
- A plan must be revised when its assumptions no longer hold.
- Early assessment and controlled experimentation support safer adaptation.
39. Q: What practical process can people use to apply storythinking to a difficult problem?
A: Show answer
A practical application of Fletcher's framework begins with immersion in the situation. Gather relevant information, observe actual behavior, and identify unexpected details that existing explanations fail to account for. Next, describe the problem in terms of the people involved, their objectives, the constraints they face, and the actions available to them. Generate alternative sequences of events: if a particular action is taken, how might other actors respond, what obstacles might arise, and what new outcomes could become possible? Compare the imagined alternatives with evidence, operational requirements, and the consequences of failure. Choose a manageable next step, test the most promising approach, and update the plan when the results differ from expectations. This process combines observation, imagination, analysis, and feedback. It is a practical synthesis of the chapter's ideas rather than a claim that Fletcher specifies one universal procedure for every situation.
Study instruction:
- Apply this process to a real challenge and document the observed problem, competing explanations, alternative plans, test, evidence, and next decision.
Key point:
- Storythinking connects observation, alternative futures, action, and revision.
- Testing and feedback are necessary to turn imagination into a reliable plan.
40. Q: How should a team evaluate an imaginative plan before committing major resources?
A: Show answer
An imaginative plan should be evaluated by examining its assumptions, mechanisms, feasibility, risks, and evidence of likely effectiveness. The team should ask what must be true for the plan to succeed, what could prevent success, whose behavior or cooperation is required, and what unintended consequences might result. It should then identify the cheapest safe way to obtain useful evidence, such as a prototype, pilot project, structured interview, or limited operational trial. Results should be compared with predetermined criteria rather than interpreted only in ways that support the idea. If the test fails, the team should determine whether the underlying concept is unsuitable, the implementation was inadequate, or the conditions differed from those expected. Some proposals cannot be tested cheaply or safely and may require more formal analysis. Creativity should therefore expand the option set while risk assessment and validation govern implementation.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page evaluation plan for a new idea, including assumptions, success measures, failure conditions, affected stakeholders, and an appropriate test.
Key point:
- Imaginative ideas require evaluation before large commitments are made.
- Well-designed tests provide evidence for refining, adopting, or rejecting a proposal.
41. Q: How should quantitative evidence be incorporated into storythinking?
A: Show answer
Quantitative evidence helps establish what has happened, measure the scale of a problem, compare outcomes, and test assumptions within defined conditions. Storythinking complements this work by asking what the evidence might mean in context, which mechanisms could explain an unexpected pattern, and what alternatives have not yet been considered. The two approaches should interact throughout a decision process. A team might use historical figures to establish current performance, investigate unusual cases to understand their causes, generate alternative plans, and then use data from a pilot to evaluate the options. Evidence should be selected according to the question, and its limitations should remain visible. A narrative that ignores contradictory data risks becoming an appealing fiction, while a dashboard used without contextual judgment may encourage narrow extrapolation. Strong decision-making combines imaginative possibility with measurement and disciplined reassessment.
Study instruction:
- Select a quantitative report and write two alternative explanations for a major finding. Specify what additional data or observations could distinguish between them.
Key point:
- Data measures patterns and helps test explanations.
- Storythinking develops hypotheses and alternatives that evidence can subsequently evaluate.
42. Q: How can organizations create a culture that supports imagination without sacrificing operational discipline?
A: Show answer
Organizations can support imagination by giving teams permission to question assumptions, investigate anomalies, propose alternatives, and learn from controlled experiments. At the same time, they need clear responsibilities, resource limits, safety requirements, approval processes, and measures of performance. These controls allow creative work to proceed without exposing the organization to unnecessary risk. Leaders should distinguish exploratory activity from approved operational commitments. Early experiments may require flexibility, while implementation must follow the relevant legal, financial, technical, and ethical requirements. Teams also need a process for evaluating unsuccessful experiments so that the lessons are captured rather than discarded. Fletcher's argument is most useful when imagination is treated as a disciplined organizational capability rather than an excuse for unsupported claims or uncontrolled experimentation. The aim is to improve the organization's ability to recognize change, discover alternatives, and implement the options that prove worthwhile.
Study instruction:
- Design a proposal process that allows early experimentation while specifying approval thresholds, documentation, risk checks, measures of success, and criteria for wider implementation.
Key point:
- Creativity and operational control can reinforce one another.
- Clear boundaries let teams explore possibilities while protecting essential requirements.
43. Q: How can storythinking be taught to people who do not consider themselves creative?
A: Show answer
Fletcher's experience suggests that people may interpret sophisticated problem-solving as a natural gift when they cannot see the methods behind it. Teaching should therefore make the method observable and give learners opportunities to practice it. An instructor can ask learners to investigate an unfamiliar situation, identify surprising details, imagine how different actions could change the outcome, and explain the assumptions behind their plans. Learners can then compare their ideas with evidence, feedback, and alternative approaches. The process should reward the quality of observation, reasoning, revision, and explanation rather than novelty alone. Some learners may find open-ended problems uncomfortable, particularly when they are accustomed to questions with one correct answer. Guided practice can help them become more comfortable with uncertainty while retaining analytical rigor. Assessment should examine both the process and the results, including whether learners recognize when their plans need to change.
Study instruction:
- Create a short training exercise in which learners investigate a novel problem, generate alternatives, test one assumption, and revise their approach based on feedback.
Key point:
- Creative problem-solving can be developed through explicit methods and deliberate practice.
- Good training rewards evidence-based exploration and thoughtful revision, not novelty alone.
44. Q: How can storythinking improve communication and collaboration across professional disciplines?
A: Show answer
Different professional groups often describe the same problem using different objectives, terminology, and measures. A narrative can help them explore how their activities interact over time and how one team's decisions affect another team's outcomes. For example, a technical team may focus on system reliability, a finance team on affordability, and an operations team on continuity of service. A shared scenario can show how a proposed technical change influences costs, workflows, training, and service delivery. This encourages participants to examine the entire sequence rather than optimize one department's performance in isolation. However, a common story must not conceal substantive disagreements or replace specialist evidence. Participants should identify whose experience is missing, which assumptions remain contested, and what decisions require formal approval. Narrative is most useful when it makes dependencies and consequences understandable while preserving the information needed for rigorous professional judgment.
Study instruction:
- Build a cross-functional scenario for an upcoming project and ask each participating team to identify its dependencies, risks, assumptions, and measures of success.
Key point:
- Shared narratives can make complex dependencies easier to understand.
- Collaboration still requires explicit evidence, responsibilities, and decisions.
45. Q: What are the limitations of storythinking, and how can people prevent imaginative reasoning from becoming overconfidence?
A: Show answer
Storythinking is useful for generating possibilities and exploring how actions might produce consequences, but it does not guarantee that the imagined sequence is accurate. People can construct compelling narratives around incomplete evidence, overlook alternatives, exaggerate their ability to predict other people's behavior, or mistake a memorable anecdote for a general rule. A convincing explanation can also encourage confirmation bias when people seek only evidence that supports the preferred interpretation. To reduce these risks, decision-makers should distinguish observations from assumptions, generate competing explanations, invite independent criticism, and identify what evidence would disprove the proposed account. Where possible, they should test plans on a limited scale and compare outcomes with predefined criteria. They should also recognize when specialist expertise, formal analysis, or established safeguards are required. Fletcher's framework is strongest when used alongside critical reasoning, quantitative evidence, and willingness to revise a preferred story.
Study instruction:
- Take a plan that initially seems convincing and construct the strongest alternative explanation, the most serious failure scenario, and the evidence that would cause you to abandon it.
Key point:
- Narrative coherence is not the same as factual accuracy or predictive reliability.
- Independent challenge, competing explanations, and testing help control overconfidence.
